# Discussion Argumentative Framework — Reverse Engineered from 15 Papers

Source papers (read in full). Papers 1–9 were read from local PDF-converted files in an earlier round; papers 10–15 were fetched live from NHESS's own HTML full-text pages (WebFetch, section-by-section, verified verbatim against citation integrity — see Step 1/Step 2 entries for each for a note on recovery completeness):
1. Yuen et al. 2021 — *Interacting effects of land-use change and natural hazards on rice agriculture in the Mekong and Red River deltas* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — literature-review / systems-thinking meta-analysis, 126 case studies synthesized into causal-loop diagrams. **Discussion = `## 4 Discussion`** (§4.1 "Untangling complexity", §4.2 "Adaptation and soft solutions"), clearly separate from `## 3 Results`.
2. Shahi et al. 2024 (pub. 2025) — *BN-FLEMOΔ: a Bayesian-network-based flood loss estimation model for adaptation planning in Ho Chi Minh City* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical survey (n=1000 households) + ML/Bayesian-network model comparison. **No standalone Discussion section — merged as `**3** **Results and discussion**`** (line 577), running through §3.1–3.3, followed directly by `**4** **Conclusion**` (line 733).
3. Nguyen et al. 2025 — *Predicting Soil Salinity in the Red River Delta (Vietnam) Using Machine Learning and Assessing Farmers' Adaptive Capacity* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical remote-sensing/ML modeling (6 XGBoost-hybrid models) + structured interviews (87 households). **Has a standalone, clearly labeled Discussion: `**5** **Discussion**`** (line 971) running to `**6** **Conclusions**` (line 1223). Note: §4.4 "Farmers' Adaptive Capacity Assessment" (inside Results, lines 779–968) carries substantial interpretive content ahead of the formal Discussion — flagged in Step 4.
4. Chounna Yemele et al. 2026 — *Land subsidence dynamics and their interplay with spatial and temporal land-use transitions in the Douala coastland, Cameroon* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical InSAR (Sentinel-1) + LULC change analysis + urban morphology correlation. **Discussion = `**5** **Discussion**`** (§5.1 "Observed rates of vertical land motion", §5.2 "Land use land cover (LULC) change", §5.3 "Land-use-driven land subsidence"), separate from `**4** **Results**`.
5. Li et al. 2021 — *Land subsidence due to groundwater pumping: hazard probability assessment through the combination of Bayesian model and fuzzy set theory* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical InSAR (PS-InSAR, 125 SAR images, 3 time periods 2003–2017) + Bayesian-network/fuzzy-set hazard model (FWBM) at the Chaobai River alluvial fan, Beijing. **No standalone Discussion section — merged as `**4** **Results and discussion**`** (line 543), running through §4.1–4.5, followed directly by `**5** **Conclusions**` (line 723).
6. Zhu et al. 2020 — *Assessments of land subsidence along the Rizhao–Lankao high-speed railway at Heze, China, between 2015 and 2019 with Sentinel-1 data* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical InSAR (SBAS/StaMPS-SB, 124 Sentinel-1 images, two tracks) mapping subsidence along a planned high-speed rail corridor and attributing it to groundwater withdrawal vs. underground coal mining. **Discussion = `**5** **Discussion**`** (§5.1 "Correlation between ground deformation and groundwater withdraw", §5.2 "Correlation between ground deformation and underground mining"), separate from `**4** **Results**` and followed by `**6** **Conclusions**`.
7. Nguyen (Nghia) et al. 2024/2025 — *Assessment of coastal inundation triggered by multiple drivers in Ca Mau Peninsula, Vietnam* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical 1D hydrodynamic modeling (MIKE11-HD, calibrated/validated) of compound-flood scenarios (upstream discharge, storm surge, riverbed lowering, land subsidence, sea-level rise, individually and combined) for the Vietnamese Mekong Delta coastal zone. **Discussion = `**4** **Discussion**`** (line 973), separate from `**3** **Results**` and followed by `**5** **Conclusions**` (line 1181).
8. Rodrigues do Amaral et al. 2023 — *Assessing typhoon-induced compound flood drivers: a case study in Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical short-timescale case study (harmonic tidal analysis, skew-surge decomposition, Manning–Strickler discharge estimation, multi-dataset rainfall comparison) of one specific typhoon event (TY Usagi, Nov 2018), data-scarce/single-event design. **Discussion = `**5** **Discussion**`** (line 1328), separate from `**4** **Results**` and followed by `**6** **Conclusions**` (line 1517).
9. Luu et al. 2024 — *Integrating susceptibility maps of multiple hazards and building exposure distribution: a case study of wildfires and floods for the province of Quang Nam, Vietnam* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical multi-hazard ML susceptibility modeling (Random Forest, CART) for floods and wildfires, combined into a susceptibility matrix and cross-referenced against building-exposure data. **Discussion = `**5** **Discussion**`** (line 854), separate from `**4** **Results**` and followed by `**6** **Conclusion**` (line 960).
10. Strozzi et al. 2026 — *Monitoring the displacement of large alpine rock slope instabilities with L-band SAR interferometric techniques* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical multi-site, multi-sensor InSAR comparison (ALOS-2 PALSAR-2, SAOCOM-1, terrestrial GLSAR vs. Sentinel-1 C-band), validated against GNSS/GB-DInSAR/MTS, at three alpine rock-slope-instability sites (Italy, Switzerland x2). **Discussion = `6 Discussion`** (§6.1 "L-band versus C-band", §6.2 "Monitoring Performance", §6.3 "Towards Multi-Frequency Integration"), separate from `5 Results` and followed by `7 Conclusion and Outlook`. Sourced live from https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/26/2579/2026/.
11. Bendoni et al. 2026 — *Multiscale modeling for coastal cities: addressing climate change impacts on flood events at urban-scale* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci., SCORE Project) — empirical multi-model coupled scenario-modeling study (WaveWatch III, SHYFEM, LISFLOOD, HEC-RAS forced by ALADIN63/EURO-CORDEX) for three European coastal cities under RCP4.5/RCP8.5. **Discussion = `7 Discussion`**, separate from `6 Results` and followed by `8 Conclusion and outlook`. Sourced live from https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/26/709/2026/.
12. Dini, Lacroix & Doin 2026 — *Beyond and beneath displacement time series: towards InSAR-based early warnings and deformation analysis of the Achoma landslide, Peru* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical single-case InSAR precursor-detection study (Sentinel-1 wrapped-phase coherence and a dimensionless activity index, cross-referenced against seismicity, rainfall, and PlanetScope-derived river erosion). **Discussion = `5 Discussion`** (§5.1–§5.5, ending "Complementarity"), separate from `4 Results` and followed by `6 Conclusions`. Sourced live from https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/26/863/2026/.
13. Cavalié, Cappa & Pinel-Puysségur 2023 — *Three decades of coastal subsidence in the slow-moving Nice Côte d'Azur Airport area (France) revealed by InSAR: insights into the deformation mechanism* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical 28-year InSAR time-series reconstruction (ERS/Envisat/Sentinel-1) plus a Burgers viscoelastic creep-model fit. **No standalone Discussion and no separate Conclusions — fully fused as `Discussion and conclusion`**, immediately following `5 Creep modelling`, with nothing after it but back-matter — a structural variant distinct from the merged-Results-and-discussion papers (which still keep a separate later Conclusions). Sourced live from https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/23/3235/2023/.
14. Barthelemy et al. 2024 — *A new approach for drought index adjustment to clay-shrinkage-induced subsidence over France: advantages of the interactive leaf area index* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical land-surface-model (ISBA/SURFEX) index-development and validation study against 19 years of French national insurance subsidence-claim data (26 municipalities). **Discussion = `4 Discussion`** (§4.1–§4.5, an unusually systematic, question-titled subsection structure culminating in a six-part uncertainty catalogue, §4.4), separate from `3 Results` and followed by `5 Conclusion`. Sourced live from https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/24/999/2024/.
15. Martins et al. 2024 — *A satellite view of the exceptionally warm summer of 2022 over Europe* (Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci.) — empirical satellite-remote-sensing (LSA SAF land-surface temperature, soil wetness, vegetation cover) and ERA5/ERA5-Land reanalysis comparison study of the 2022 European heatwave. **No section named "Discussion" and no "Results and discussion" section at all — interpretive/limitation content is split between the tail of `3 Results` (§3.5) and a `4 Conclusions` section that performs the full range of Discussion-type work** without ever using Discussion-typical section language — its own distinct structural category, counted separately from every other paper's position-testability classification. Sourced live from https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/24/1501/2024/.

---

## STEP 1 — Per-Paper Foundations

### Paper 1: Yuen et al. 2021 (rice agriculture, Mekong/Red River deltas)
- **Research question:** How do anthropogenic land-use drivers and natural hazards interact, at multiple scales, to affect rice growing area, yield, and quality in Vietnam's two deltas?
- **Objective:** Build a systems-thinking, causal-loop-diagram synthesis (not a new empirical measurement) that shows the interdependence of drivers, using existing literature as the evidence base.
- **Study design:** Meta-analytic literature review. 126 articles identified via database search (Scopus, Web of Science, Google Scholar, grey literature), each read and coded for causal links (positive/negative polarity) between drivers and rice outcomes.
- **Population/sample:** Not a population in the empirical sense — the "sample" is the corpus of 126 case-study articles.
- **Key findings the Discussion must interpret:**
  - Local anthropogenic drivers (aquaculture/crop shift, urban expansion, dikes, sand mining, groundwater extraction) each reduce rice growing area or quality through distinct, documented mechanisms.
  - Regional driver (upstream dams) reduces sediment supply even at large distances from the deltas.
  - Natural hazards (drought, flood, typhoon, pests/disease, sea-level rise) damage rice independently but also interact with and amplify anthropogenic problems (e.g., groundwater-driven subsidence worsens saltwater intrusion, which is separately worsened by drought and sea-level rise).
  - Some anthropogenic interventions (dikes, agrochemicals) that solve one problem create new ones (sediment starvation, pesticide resistance) — a "wicked problem" structure.

### Paper 2: Shahi et al. 2024/2025 (BN-FLEMOΔ, flood loss, Ho Chi Minh City)
- **Research question:** Can a Bayesian-network model, built from empirical survey data with automatic feature selection, outperform existing flood-loss estimation approaches while also quantifying uncertainty and tolerating missing predictors?
- **Objective:** Develop and validate the first probabilistic multi-variable flood-loss model for residential buildings in a delta city (HCMC), designed for adaptation-planning use.
- **Study design:** Cross-sectional household survey (retrospective self-report of flood damage) + automated ML feature selection (SelectKBest) + Bayesian network structure learning (hill-climbing selected as best) + 10-fold cross-validation model comparison against SDF-Det, SDF-Prob, Random Forest, and Bayesian Regression.
- **Population/sample:** 1000 households in HCMC that experienced flood damage in the prior 10 years (2010–2020); 1530 flood-event records after some households reported two events; reduced to 1063 complete-case records after excluding missing values.
- **Key findings the Discussion (merged into §3, "Results and discussion") must interpret:**
  - 7 of 16 candidate predictors are optimal (water depth, flood experience, building elevation, sewage contamination, years-since-renovation, warning, inundation duration); water depth ranks first, consistent with prior literature.
  - The Markov blanket of relative loss = {water depth, flood experience, years-since-renovation}; when renovation data is missing, building elevation substitutes.
  - BN-FLEMOΔ achieves lowest MAE (0.18±0.01) and lowest mean CRPS (0.11±0.01) among five compared models; RF performs worst and overestimates.
  - Predictive uncertainty is lower for low-loss buildings than for high-loss (near-total-damage) buildings, attributed to data scarcity at the extreme.
  - A discrepancy versus Chinh et al. (2017) in Can Tho: that study found flood duration much more important than this study does.

### Paper 3: Nguyen et al. 2025 (soil salinity, Red River Delta / Thai Binh)
- **Research question:** (a) Can machine learning (XGBoost + five metaheuristic optimizers) combined with Sentinel-1/2 remote sensing accurately map soil salinity in the understudied Red River Delta? (b) What is the adaptive capacity of farming households to soil salinity in a representative coastal commune?
- **Objective:** Build an integrated theoretical framework combining spatial salinity prediction with a five-capital (natural/human/physical/financial/social) adaptive-capacity assessment, to support policy and farmer decision-making.
- **Study design:** (a) In-situ EC sampling (62 soil samples, dry season 2024) + Sentinel-1/Sentinel-2A derived spectral/radar indices + Random Forest feature importance + 6 XGBoost-based hybrid models (XGB alone, and XGB-POA/STO/SOA/PSO/GOA), evaluated via RMSE/MAE/R² on a 70/30 train-validation split. (b) Structured interviews with 87 randomly selected households in An Tan commune, Thai Thuy district.
- **Population/sample:** 30 million pixels (10×10 m) for the salinity map; 87 households for the adaptive-capacity survey.
- **Key findings the Discussion (standalone `§5 Discussion`) must interpret:**
  - XGB-POA is the best model (R²=0.968, RMSE=0.31, MAE=0.242 on validation), outperforming XGB-STO, XGB-SOA, XGB-PSO, XGB-GOA, and outperforming comparator studies elsewhere (Wang & Sun 2024 R²=0.84; Aksoy et al. 2024 R²=0.83; Elshewy et al. 2024 R²=0.86).
  - Salinity is spatially concentrated in coastal/riverside zones; ~165 km² of rice land is "slightly" affected, 1.8 km² "greatly" affected (mostly aquaculture, so productivity impact is limited there).
  - Adaptive capacity is constrained primarily by financial and human-capital limitations (low education, unstable income, capital-access barriers), despite long farming experience.
  - A causal chain is asserted for worsening salinity: upstream dams reduce dry-season discharge → reduced river's capacity to repel seawater → combined with sea-level rise and groundwater-extraction-driven subsidence.

### Paper 4: Chounna Yemele et al. 2026 (InSAR land subsidence, Douala, Cameroon)
- **Research question:** What are the rates and spatial pattern of vertical land motion (VLM) in the Douala coastland, and how do they relate to land-use/land-cover (LULC) change and urban morphology (building height, function, timing of urbanization)?
- **Objective:** Fill a documented data gap (no prior local-scale subsidence study for Cameroon's coast) by quantifying VLM via InSAR and statistically relating it to LULC transition history and urban building characteristics.
- **Study design:** Sentinel-1 SBAS-type InSAR time series (2018–2023, 313 images) → VLM map referenced to IGS14 global frame; LULC maps from ESA (1992–2015) and Esri (2017–2022) reclassified into 7 classes; four LULC-to-built-up transition "paths" (from cropland, rangeland, wetland, tree cover) tested for correlation with subsidence rate; GHSL urban morphology layers (building height, volume, population density) correlated with VLM within Douala city.
- **Population/sample:** 68,680 InSAR scattering points across a 2700 km² study area; no human survey component.
- **Key findings the Discussion (standalone `§5 Discussion`) must interpret:**
  - 97.9% of scattering points show subsidence; mean rate −2.7 mm/yr, range −17.6 to +3.8 mm/yr.
  - Areas that urbanized most recently (2017–2022, e.g., from tree cover) show the highest subsidence rates (up to 7.1 mm/yr median); areas urbanized earliest (built-up since 1992) show the lowest (1.9 mm/yr) — an inverse relationship between subsidence rate and time-since-urbanization.
  - Subsidence rate decreases with increasing building height (3–6 m buildings subside most; taller buildings least), attributed to deep foundations bypassing shallow compressible soils.
  - 2.1% of points show apparent uplift, concentrated in an undisturbed wetland area — flagged as possibly a processing artifact (unresolved N-S motion) or a genuine sedimentation signal.

### Paper 5: Li et al. 2021 (FWBM hazard probability, Chaobai River alluvial fan, Beijing)
- **Research question:** Can a hazard-probability model that combines a weighted Bayesian network with fuzzy set theory (to represent the ambiguity in how important each contributing factor is) produce a more defensible, quantitatively updatable land-subsidence hazard map than existing qualitative/expert-score methods?
- **Objective:** Build and demonstrate a Fuzzy Weighted Bayesian Model (FWBM) that (a) infers hazard probability from four factors (confined-aquifer groundwater-level-change rate, unconfined-aquifer rate, compressible-sediment thickness, Quaternary-unit thickness), (b) uses InSAR-derived subsidence as the observational input to update posterior probability, and (c) is validated against independent InSAR subsidence-rate-change data.
- **Study design:** Empirical case study, not an experiment — PS-InSAR processing of 125 SAR images (37 ASAR 2003–2010, 38 RADARSAT-2 2010–2014, 50 Sentinel-1 2014–2017) gridded into 5664 cells (500 m × 500 m); Bayesian hazard-probability inference per factor, combined via fuzzy-weighted aggregation (weights derived by fuzzy-extended AHP); results reclassified into 7 hazard grades and cross-validated against the sign of InSAR-observed subsidence-rate change (2010–2014 vs. 2015–2017) using a match-ratio statistic; a non-fuzzy WBM run in parallel as an ablation/comparison.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — spatial units are the 5664 grid cells (1350 km² study area) in the upper-middle Chaobai River alluvial fan, Beijing.
- **Key findings the Discussion (merged into §4, "Results and discussion") must interpret:**
  - FWBM achieves an 85% total match ratio against independent InSAR subsidence-rate-change validation, versus 82% for the non-fuzzy WBM ablation.
  - Groundwater-level decline rate >1 m/yr in confined and unconfined aquifers yields maximum hazard probabilities of 0.65 and 0.68, respectively; compressible-sediment thickness 160–170 m yields 0.32; Quaternary thickness 400–500 m yields 0.35 — the latter two probabilities decrease again at the very largest thickness values (a non-monotonic pattern requiring explanation).
  - Overall study-area hazard probability decreased from 51.3% (2003–2010) to 28.3% (2015–2017), attributed to the 2014 South-to-North Water Transfer Project reducing aquifer drawdown pressure.
  - FWBM and WBM diverge spatially: WBM overestimates hazard in areas where subsidence rate later decreased and underestimates it where the rate increased, i.e., fuzzy weighting improves directional accuracy, not just aggregate match rate.

### Paper 6: Zhu et al. 2020 (SBAS-InSAR subsidence, Rizhao–Lankao high-speed railway, Heze, China)
- **Research question:** What is the spatial pattern and rate of ground subsidence along the under-construction Rizhao–Lankao high-speed railway corridor at Heze (2015–2019), and what is causing it — groundwater extraction, underground coal mining, or both?
- **Objective:** Map contemporary deformation along the 150 km railway corridor using an improved multi-temporal InSAR technique (adaptive-spatial-filtering StaMPS-SB) at high point density (to compensate for the corridor's mostly rural, low-coherence farmland setting), and attribute the observed subsidence zones to their physical drivers to inform railway safety planning.
- **Study design:** Empirical remote-sensing case study — 124 Sentinel-1A SAR images (63 track S1-40, 61 track S1-142, July 2015–November 2019) processed with an adaptive-filtering variant of StaMPS-SB (small baseline subset); results cross-validated between the two independent tracks (Pearson r, RMSE); displacement time series extracted at labeled rural/urban/mining points and compared against groundwater-level records and monthly precipitation.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — spatial measurement points (232,984 common MPs after cross-track gridding; up to ~473 MPs/km² along the corridor) across a study area centered on the Heze region, North China Plain.
- **Key findings the Discussion (standalone `§5 Discussion`) must interpret:**
  - Two subsidence zones intersect the railway: Zone 1 (east of Heze city), −4 to −1 cm/yr, ~20 km of corridor affected; Zone 2 (coalfield), −8 to −2 cm/yr, ~15 km affected.
  - Cross-track validation: Pearson r = 0.9, RMSE = 0.5 cm/yr between the two independently processed tracks, establishing ±0.5 cm/yr precision.
  - Rural-area displacement shows a ~2-month-lagged seasonal correlation with precipitation (attributed to shallow-aquifer recharge cycling); urban-area displacement shows no such relationship (attributed to deep-groundwater extraction, which the shallow recharge cycle cannot reach).
  - A "secondary" subsidence basin appears 3–7 km from the coalfield's primary (directly mining-induced) subsidence basin; its linear (not bowl-shaped, not high-rate) displacement pattern is inconsistent with direct mining-induced subsidence, leading the authors to infer an indirect cause (groundwater outflow through mining-induced fractures, and/or fault reactivation).

### Paper 7: Nguyen (Nghia) et al. 2024/2025 (compound coastal inundation, Ca Mau Peninsula, Vietnam)
- **Research question:** Among upstream high flow, storm surge, human-induced riverbed lowering, land subsidence, and sea-level rise (individually and combined), which drivers actually control present and future inundation extent/depth in the Ca Mau Peninsula (CMP), the most flood-prone coastal region of the Vietnamese Mekong Delta?
- **Objective:** Build an updated, better-calibrated 1D hydrodynamic model of the whole Mekong Delta (with 2018–2019 dyke and riverbed data) to generate CMP-specific inundation maps under single-driver and combined-driver future scenarios, providing a decision-support basis for infrastructure and adaptation planning.
- **Study design:** Empirical scenario-modeling study — MIKE11-HD 1D hydrodynamic model (~4235 river branches, 564 floodplain compartments) calibrated against a high-flow year (2018) and validated against a low-flow year (2016) using Nash–Sutcliffe efficiency and R²; 10 scenarios run (1 baseline, 5 single-driver, 2 further single-driver, 2 combined-driver) varying upstream discharge, storm surge, riverbed bathymetry, land subsidence (from Minderhoud et al. 2020 projections), and sea-level rise (IPCC AR6 SSP5-8.5); outputs converted to gridded inundation-depth maps (5 m DEM) and area statistics by depth class.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — the ~5210 km² CMP land area and ~30,000 hydrodynamic-model computational nodes; no survey component.
- **Key findings the Discussion (standalone `§4 Discussion`) must interpret:**
  - Riverbed lowering (S3) and storm surge on the mainstream Mekong (S2) produce negligible change in CMP inundation relative to baseline; high upstream flow alone (S1) produces only a small rise (0.1–0.3 m).
  - Land subsidence and sea-level rise are the dominant drivers: by 2050 (S4_a, moderate subsidence) mean inundation depth rises from 0.04 m (baseline) to 0.42 m; by 2100 (S4_b, severe subsidence) to 1.15 m; sea-level rise scenarios (S5_a/S5_b) produce comparable or larger increases.
  - Combined-driver scenarios project 96% of the CMP inundated by mid-century (S6, mean depth 0.91 m) and 99% inundated by end-of-century (S7, mean depth 2.20 m) under SSP5-8.5.
  - Projected land subsidence (0.77 m by 2050, 2.34 m by 2100) is large enough, combined with projected peak water levels, to bring the design road-crest elevation of the North–South Expressway below future flood levels by end-of-century.

### Paper 8: Rodrigues do Amaral et al. 2023 (typhoon-induced compound flood drivers, Ho Chi Minh City)
- **Research question:** During the single most extreme rainfall event on record in Ho Chi Minh City (Typhoon Usagi, 25 Nov 2018), which physical drivers — storm surge, river discharge, or rainfall/tidal interaction — actually controlled the resulting flooding, at sub-monthly (event) timescale?
- **Objective:** Provide an observation-based, multi-method (harmonic tidal analysis, skew-surge decomposition, Manning–Strickler-derived discharge, multi-product rainfall comparison) reconstruction of the hydrosystem's short-timescale behavior during and after TY Usagi, in a data-scarce region where long-duration statistical compound-flood analysis is not feasible.
- **Study design:** Single-event empirical case study (not a statistical/probabilistic compound-flood model) — in-situ water-level sensors at coastal and upstream/urban river stations (rare pre-existing instrumentation operating through the event) analyzed via harmonic tidal decomposition to isolate storm surge; discharge estimated via a modified Manning–Strickler law from water-level slope; five gridded/satellite rainfall products (including MSWEP, ERA5) compared against in-situ rainfall; MODIS-derived flood-extent imagery used qualitatively.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — a single hydrometeorological event (TY Usagi) observed at two river gauge stations (upstream/Phu Cuong, urban) and one coastal station on the Saigon River system, Ho Chi Minh City.
- **Key findings the Discussion (standalone `§5 Discussion`) must interpret:**
  - No significant coastal storm surge occurred (explained by TY Usagi's landfall angle producing offshore, not onshore, winds), but a statistically significant river surge did occur — inland rainfall-driven forcing dominated over coastal forcing for this specific event.
  - A 16 h lag between peak rainfall and peak residual river discharge, attributed to flat terrain, vegetation interception, upstream flooding, and possible shallow-aquifer recharge buffering.
  - Only 9.3% of available rainwater was evacuated as residual river discharge 41 h after the rain event ended, consistent with evapotranspiration dominating the local water budget (cross-checked against an independent water-budget model, Rujner and Goedecke 2016).
  - MSWEP was the most accurate of the tested rainfall products for this event; ERA5 was the worst (systematic wet bias), consistent with prior literature on ERA5's known tropical/reanalysis limitations.

### Paper 9: Luu et al. 2024 (multi-hazard susceptibility and building exposure, Quang Nam, Vietnam)
- **Research question:** How can flood and wildfire susceptibility be jointly mapped and combined with building-exposure data to produce a multi-hazard exposure profile for a province facing both hazards on different spatial footprints and seasonal timing?
- **Objective:** Build separate Random Forest/CART susceptibility models for floods and wildfires in Quang Nam province, integrate them into a semi-quantitative multi-hazard susceptibility matrix (rather than a simple summed index), and overlay building-location data to quantify differential exposure.
- **Study design:** Empirical ML case study — geospatial database of hazard inventories (flood and wildfire occurrence points), topographic/geologic/hydrologic/climatic predictors, and building footprints; Random Forest and CART susceptibility models trained separately for each hazard and evaluated by ROC-AUC on held-out validation data; susceptibility classes combined via a matrix (not additive/weighted-sum) into 4 combined classes; building counts and areas cross-tabulated against the resulting multi-hazard matrix cells.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — the province of Quang Nam, Central Vietnam (building footprints and grid cells as the exposure units); no survey component.
- **Key findings the Discussion (standalone `§5 Discussion`) must interpret:**
  - Random Forest outperforms CART for both hazards (flood AUC 0.882 vs. lower CART value; wildfire AUC 0.884 vs. 0.846).
  - The multi-hazard matrix shows an inverse spatial relationship: areas of very high wildfire susceptibility have low flood susceptibility and vice versa (lowland coastal = flood-dominant; mid-altitude slopes = fire-dominant); no grid cell is jointly high-flood/high-fire.
  - Building exposure is disproportionate to area: the fraction of buildings in the high-flood/low-fire category is much larger than that category's area share, while the fraction of buildings in the low-flood/very-high-fire category is much smaller than its area share — flood exposure to buildings is more consequential than the raw susceptibility footprint alone would suggest.
  - Independent (non-spatially-resolved) provincial damage statistics (2020 flood: 46 deaths, >117,000 properties damaged; 5-year wildfire record: 136 fires, >618 ha burned) are consistent in direction with the modeled flood-exposure-exceeds-fire-exposure finding, but cannot be spatially cross-validated against the exposure map due to their coarse (non-gridded) reporting.

---

## STEP 2 — Per-Paper Discussion Paragraph-by-Paragraph Analysis

### PAPER 1 — Yuen et al. 2021, `§4 Discussion`

#### §4.1 "Untangling complexity" — Paragraph 1 (lines 191)
1. **Function:** Opening/synthesizing paragraph — restates and consolidates the Results' causal-diagram findings into a single integrated narrative, moving from the disaggregated per-driver Results sections to a combined systems view.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The full set of local anthropogenic driver→outcome links documented in §3.1 (aquaculture/crops, urban expansion, dikes, sand mining, groundwater extraction).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** No single hedge phrase — the move is *aggregative restatement*: each sentence chains one driver to its established downstream effect ("Rice growing areas are negatively affected by...", "Dikes keep floodwaters... but there is a reduction..."). The paragraph performs synthesis by sequential chaining of already-established causal links rather than introducing new interpretation.
4. **Literature use:** None introduced in this paragraph — it is pure synthesis of the paper's own Results.
5. **Explanation/mechanism certainty:** Not applicable in the "propose new mechanism" sense — this paragraph reports what the diagrams (built from 126 studies) already established as documented causal links, not new speculation. Certainty is inherited from the underlying case studies, not assigned here.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A — no comparison to expectation; this is descriptive synthesis.
7. **Interpretation → implication move:** Implicit only — sets up the "wicked problem" framing that is made explicit in the next paragraph.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role in flow:** Opening/orienting — consolidates before generalizing.

#### §4.1 — Paragraph 2 (lines 193)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — extends the synthesis to natural hazards and explicitly names the *amplification* dynamic between natural and anthropogenic drivers.
2. **Finding it starts from:** §3.3/3.4 natural-hazard findings (drought, typhoon, sea-level rise) and their documented interaction with anthropogenic salinity/subsidence drivers.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Signal phrase "Natural hazards not only affect rice quality and quantity but **may also amplify**..." — an explicit amplification claim, immediately followed by concrete instances ("typhoons and sea level rise may induce saltwater flooding and aggravate salinity intrusion. Conversely, droughts also worsen..."). The hedge word "may" appears twice, signaling a plausible-mechanism rather than an established-fact certainty level.
4. **Literature use:** Implicit citation of Hausfather et al. 2017, Grosjean et al. 2016, Terry et al. 2012 (bundled as "*cf.*") to support a forward-looking claim that climate change will increase hazard frequency/severity — literature used to *extend* the claim into a future projection, not to test agreement/disagreement.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly hedged ("may... may also amplify," "may intensify") — self-flagged as plausible mechanism, not demonstrated fact, appropriate given the review's non-experimental design.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** The implication is forward-looking/projective ("problems... may intensify") rather than policy-prescriptive — this paragraph's job is to extend the causal picture into future climate-change conditions, with the practical implications deferred to §4.2.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty:** The hedging *is* the limitation-bounding mechanism here — uncertainty is built into the claim's phrasing rather than stated separately.
9. **Role in flow:** Core interpretive — extrapolates the established pattern.

#### §4.1 — Paragraph 3 (lines 195)
1. **Function:** Qualifying/scoping — introduces heterogeneity across the two deltas, cautioning against over-generalizing the systems diagram.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The diagram's implicit assumption that drivers apply "equally" to both deltas.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explicit caution marker: "we caution that... there are differences in the ways each delta is affected." Followed by concrete asymmetries (high dikes unique to MRD; RRD has far fewer dams — 105 vs. 361; typhoons rarer in MRD, droughts rarer in RRD).
4. **Literature use:** Chapman et al. (2017) cited to support that high dikes/sediment exclusion is MRD-specific — literature deployed to *qualify* (bound the scope of) the general claim, not to support or contrast a causal mechanism.
5. **Certainty level:** These are stated as established facts (dam counts, presence/absence of high dikes), not hedged — because they are structural/geographic facts, not causal inferences.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** Implicit: readers should not treat the flow diagrams as spatially uniform.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** This entire paragraph functions as a scope-limitation on the generalizability of §4.1's synthesis.
9. **Role in flow:** Qualifying/bounding — interrupts the synthesis to prevent overclaiming uniformity.

#### §4.1 — Paragraph 4 (lines 197)
1. **Function:** Further qualification with quantitative sub-delta evidence — narrows the geographic claim down to within-delta variability.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Continuation of the heterogeneity theme, now at finer spatial grain (arsenic hotspots, salinity gradients).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Comparative citation of two studies' *numeric ranges*: Berg et al. (2007) arsenic concentrations (1–845 µg/L MRD vs. 1–3050 µg/L RRD) and Kotera et al. (2005) vs. an unnamed Nam Dinh study on salinity gradients — juxtaposed to demonstrate spatial non-uniformity within each delta.
4. **Literature use:** Berg et al. (2007) and Kotera et al. (2005) used as *evidence sources* for the magnitude/pattern of within-delta variation — a supporting rather than contrasting use.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established empirical fact (measured concentrations), attributed directly to the cited studies.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A (no discordance being resolved).
7. **Interpretation → implication:** Explicit closing statement: "it is important for local conditions to be taken into consideration" — a direct policy/practice implication drawn from the heterogeneity evidence.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty:** Continues the scope-bounding function of paragraph 3.
9. **Role in flow:** Qualifying, transitioning toward limitations.

#### §4.1 — Paragraph 5 (lines 199)
1. **Function:** Explicit limitations paragraph, followed immediately by a defense/reframing of the paper's contribution and a worked practical example.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own scope choice (rice-focused causal diagrams).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct limitation statement: "One limitation of our study is that it was not possible to include all the problems..." — names a specific omitted factor (industrial pollution) with supporting citations (Khai and Yabe 2012, 2021; Huong et al. 2008). This is immediately followed by a pivot ("In spite of this...") that reasserts the value of the systems-thinking method despite the limitation — a defend-then-extend move rather than a paragraph that ends on the limitation.
4. **Literature use:** Khai and Yabe (2012, 2021), Huong et al. (2008) cited to substantiate that the omitted factor is real and documented elsewhere — supporting the limitation's plausibility, not contrasting the paper's findings.
5. **Certainty level:** The limitation itself is stated plainly (fact about scope), not hedged; the omitted mechanism (industrial pollution reducing rice quality/productivity) is attributed to the cited studies, not claimed by this paper.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** Ends with a concrete applied example (a hypothetical fix for "shrinking delta" requiring joint consideration of dikes, sand mining, dams, and groundwater extraction) and cites DeFries and Nagendra (2017) on the danger of oversimplified solutions to wicked problems — this is the paragraph's practical-implication payload.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty:** This paragraph *is* the limitations statement, notable for being placed mid-Discussion (not at the very end) and for being immediately counterbalanced rather than left as an unresolved caveat.
9. **Role in flow:** Limitations, interleaved with reassertion of contribution — deviates from the "limitations only at the very end" textbook pattern (see Step 4).

#### §4.2 "Adaptation and soft solutions" — Paragraphs 6–11 (lines 203–213)
This subsection is thematically distinct: it shifts from *explaining the problem* (§4.1) to *cataloguing existing/possible responses* (§4.2), effectively functioning as an implications-and-recommendations block structured as a second mini-argument.

- **Para 6 (203):** Function = positive-adaptation synthesis. Starts from farmer behavior findings (crop switching, rice-shrimp rotation, elevated planting). Move: descriptive chaining with no explicit hedge — frames farmer adaptation as demonstrated coping ("farmers have adapted and improvised"). Literature (Tran et al. 2019; Nguyen et al. 2012) used to support the claim that farmers experiment and share knowledge. Role: opens the implications subsection with a hopeful framing.
- **Para 7 (205):** Function = counterbalancing discordant/unintended-consequence finding. Explicit pivot marker: "Unfortunately, adaptations are not successful all the time." Cites Leigh et al. (2017) — a *contrasting* use of literature, showing rice-shrimp systems can fail (only 3/18 ponds produced harvestable rice). This is a clear discordant-finding-handling paragraph: it does not dismiss the counter-evidence but incorporates it to qualify the optimism of Para 6.
- **Para 8 (207):** Function = extending the solutions catalogue (integrated pest management). Mechanism: "should also be adopted" — a normative/prescriptive verb signaling a practice recommendation rather than a described finding. Literature (Bottrell and Schoenly 2012; Normile 2013) supports feasibility.
- **Para 9 (209):** Function = mechanistic elaboration of one adaptation (rice-fish, duck-rice) with quantified benefit (up to 30%, 50–150% income increase, Men et al. 2002). Certainty: stated as demonstrated outcome from a cited empirical study, not this paper's own data — properly attributed.
- **Para 10 (211):** Function = discordant-finding handling again — a policy recommendation (move from triple- to double-cropping) is reported as *not adopted in practice*, citing Tran and Weger (2018): "most farmers have largely ignored the directive." This is an explicit acknowledgment that a plausible/recommended solution fails against real behavioral incentives — closes with an evaluative statement ("farmers are willing to make changes... only if it benefits them") that reads as an inferential conclusion grounded in the cited study.
- **Para 11 (213):** Function = closing/synthesizing paragraph for the whole Discussion — elevates from specific adaptations to a general policy stance ("need for holistic land-use planning and soft measures... on top of hard engineering"). Cites Neumann et al. (2015), Smajgl et al. (2015), Hoang et al. (2018), Geist and Lambin (2002) to support the critique of hard-engineering-only approaches. Ends by explicitly returning to the paper's stated method (systems-thinking) as the recommended tool — a closing-loop move that ties the Discussion back to the Introduction's stated approach.

### PAPER 2 — Shahi et al. 2024/2025, `§3 Results and discussion` (merged — no standalone Discussion)

Because Results and Discussion are merged, each subsection mixes result-reporting with interpretation. The paragraphs below are drawn from the interpretive portions.

#### §3.1 "Important variables for flood loss modelling" — interpretive paragraph (lines 583–607)
1. **Function:** Result-to-interpretation within a merged section — reports the feature-selection outcome, then interprets each top feature's role by connecting to established loss theory.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The SelectKBest result: 7 optimal features, water depth top-ranked (MSE≈0.0719 at k=7).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Comparison-to-literature signal phrase: "Water depth is found to be the most important driver for flood loss, which **is in agreement with** comprehensive reviews and state-of-the-art flood loss models (Merz et al., 2013; Chinh et al., 2017; Rözer et al., 2019; Merz et al., 2010)." This is a direct concordance statement — the finding matches prior consensus, and the paragraph uses that agreement as validation rather than needing new mechanistic explanation.
4. **Literature use:** Multiple citations bundled to support concordance (four citations after "agreement with"); then, feature-by-feature, additional citations are used to explain *why* each feature matters mechanistically (flood experience → precautionary elevation, citing Vishwanath Harish et al. 2023, Kreibich et al. 2005, Chinh et al. 2017; sewage contamination → damage increase, citing Rözer et al. 2019, Thieken et al. 2005, Penning-Rowsell et al. 2014; renovation recency → building quality degradation, citing Chinh et al. 2017).
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established, literature-concordant fact ("is in agreement with," "has been shown before that it leads to..." — Chinh et al. 2017 cited for a definite claim, not hedged).
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant — this paragraph's entire interpretive strategy is demonstrating alignment with prior work, feature by feature.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** Minimal explicit implication in this paragraph — implications are deferred to §3.2 and the Conclusion.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** None here.
9. **Role in flow:** Core interpretive, comparative — validates the automated feature-selection method by anchoring it against known theory.

#### §3.2 "BN-FLEMOΔ" — interpretive paragraph (lines 610–652)
1. **Function:** Interpretation of network structure + one explicit discordant comparison with a highly similar prior study.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The Markov blanket result (wd, fe, renov as direct predictors of rloss) and the model's tolerance for missing "renov" via "elev" as a substitute.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Concordance stated first ("is aligned with flood loss dynamics and prior research findings (Chinh et al., 2017)"), then an explicit discordance flagged: "**Interestingly, we also observe some differences compared to** a somewhat similar study by Chinh et al. (2017) in Can Tho City, Mekong Delta; they found a significantly higher importance of flood duration." The signal phrase "interestingly... differences compared to" explicitly marks discordance.
4. **Literature use:** Chinh et al. (2017) used first to *support* concordance, then re-cited in the same paragraph to *contrast* — an unusual dual use of a single source, both validating and complicating the finding.
5. **Certainty level:** Not heavily hedged — the discordance is stated as an observed fact, and its resolution ("These differences... confirm the need for region- and flood-type-specific loss models") is offered as an explanatory framework rather than a fully tested mechanism, citing Wagenaar et al. (2018) and Mohor et al. (2021) as support.
6. **Discordant-finding handling:** This is the clearest discordance-handling paragraph in the corpus for this paper: the difference is not explained away as error but reframed as evidence *for* a broader claim (loss models are inherently region/flood-type specific) — turning a potential weakness into a generalizable insight.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** Practical implication follows directly: because elev is easier to observe than renov (e.g., via Google Street View, citing Pelizari et al. 2021), the model's practical applicability is improved — a direct bridge from a structural/statistical finding to field-deployability.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty:** Discussed implicitly via the missing-data handling capability, framed as strength rather than limitation.
9. **Role in flow:** Core interpretive + comparative/discordant — the paper's central "here's what makes our model useful in practice" argument.

#### §3.2 continued — uncertainty paragraph (lines 653–676)
1. **Function:** Interpreting the model's probabilistic-uncertainty output, including an unfavorable pattern (worse performance at extremes) and its cause.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Fig. 4 predictive densities — narrower/more accurate distributions for low-loss buildings, wider/less certain for high-loss (near-total-damage) buildings.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct causal attribution with hedge: "Such an observation **is due to** the scarcity of extreme losses in the dataset." This is stated fairly directly (not heavily hedged), but it is a data-availability explanation rather than a claim about the underlying physical/social damage process — an epistemic-uncertainty explanation, appropriately matched to a sampling-limitation cause.
4. **Literature use:** None in this specific sub-paragraph — self-contained data-based reasoning.
5. **Certainty level:** Presented as the accepted proximate explanation, not merely speculative, but it is a *data-limitation* explanation rather than a domain-mechanism claim, which is an appropriate epistemic move (does not overclaim about damage physics).
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A — this is explaining an internal model behavior, not comparing to other studies.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** Implicit — signals a boundary condition on the model's practical reliability for catastrophic-loss cases.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** This entire paragraph functions as an interleaved limitation (rather than being deferred to a separate "Limitations" section) — again showing the interleaved-not-terminal pattern also seen in Yuen.
9. **Role in flow:** Mechanistic/limitations-interleaved.

#### §3.3 "Model validation" — comparative paragraph (lines 693–711)
1. **Function:** Head-to-head quantitative model comparison and final positioning of the proposed model relative to four alternatives.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Fig. 5 metrics: BN-FLEMOΔ lowest MAE (0.18±0.01) and mean CRPS (0.11±0.01); RF weakest overall; BR/SDF-Prob/SDF-Det roughly comparable to each other.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct numeric comparison stated as fact, with an explicit concession: "**Despite partly marginal differences** between BN-FLEMOΔ and BR, SDF-Prob, and SDF-Det, the advantages of our model are..." — this concession-then-pivot is the key rhetorical device: the paper does not overclaim decisive numeric superiority, instead pivoting the argument to *qualitative* advantages (missing-data tolerance, uncertainty quantification, graphical interpretability) that the metrics alone don't capture.
4. **Literature use:** None new here — self-referential to the paper's own comparison design (§2.3.1).
5. **Certainty level:** Numeric claims stated as fact (measured metrics); the *interpretive* claim ("advantages... are...") is presented as reasoned argument, appropriately calibrated to not overclaim beyond what "marginal" numeric differences would support alone.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Handles a "null/marginal" result honestly — rather than inflating a small numeric edge, the paper explicitly names it as marginal and redirects the argument to non-numeric advantages.
7. **Interpretation → implication:** This IS the implication paragraph for the model-comparison thread — leads directly into the Conclusion's claims about decision-support value.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The "marginal differences" concession functions as a self-imposed limitation on how strongly the paper can claim performance superiority.
9. **Role in flow:** Comparative, closing this section.

### PAPER 3 — Nguyen et al. 2025, `§5 Discussion` (standalone)

#### Paragraph 1 (lines 974–980)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting — reasserts the global and Vietnam-specific significance of the problem, framing why this Discussion matters.
2. **Finding it starts from:** No specific numeric finding yet — a framing statement referencing the study's motivation.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** General significance framing citing Song et al. (2024); no specific finding-to-interpretation move yet — this paragraph is pure orientation.
4. **Literature use:** Song et al. (2024) supports the general problem-severity claim.
5. **Certainty level:** N/A (general statement, not this paper's finding).
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** N/A yet.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting.

#### Paragraph 2 (lines 981–991)
1. **Function:** Positioning the study within the literature gap — explicit gap statement.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own contribution (RRD, understudied vs. Mekong Delta).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct gap-naming: "Research on the Red River Delta is scarce," contrasted against Mekong Delta studies (Nguyen et al. 2024; Hoang and Hai 2024) — a *contrastive* literature use establishing novelty by omission (what's missing from prior work) rather than by disagreement with existing findings.
4. **Literature use:** Nguyen et al. (2024), Hoang and Hai (2024) cited to show what has been done (elsewhere), implicitly contrasted with what has not (RRD).
5. **Certainty level:** Factual/scope statement, not hedged.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A — gap-framing, not agreement/disagreement.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — justifies the paper's approach (combining RS + ML + interviews) as filling the gap.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting, transitioning to core interpretive material.

#### Paragraph 3 (lines 992–1009)
1. **Function:** Mechanistic explanation of *why* the chosen remote-sensing inputs (Sentinel-1 + Sentinel-2) work, and a mid-paragraph acknowledgment of a method limitation with the proposed fix already built into the study design.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's variable-importance results (VVVH, VV, distance-to-river as top predictors, from §4.1).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explanatory chaining ("Soils with different salinity levels will have different spectral characteristics... This is the basis for using remote sensors...") followed by an explicit limitation-then-solution move: "**However, one of the challenges** in using Sentinel 2 satellite images... is that sometimes, the spectral reflectance level is not consistent with soil salinity... Therefore, this study has integrated Sentinel 1 images..." — this is a distinctive rhetorical pattern: naming a known weakness of a common method and then explaining how the paper's own design choice (combining SAR with optical) mitigates it.
4. **Literature use:** Hoa et al. (2019), Wu et al. (2018), Xiao et al. (2023) support the spectral-salinity relationship claim.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated fairly directly as established remote-sensing physics, not heavily hedged, appropriate since this is describing known sensor behavior rather than a new causal claim about the world.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The implication is methodological: justifies the paper's dual-sensor design choice as superior to single-sensor optical approaches.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** A limitation of the *general method* (optical remote sensing) is named and then shown to be addressed by this paper's specific design — a "limitation-as-motivation" move distinct from a closing caveat.
9. **Role:** Mechanistic — explains the "why it works" behind the top-level results.

#### Paragraph 4 (lines 1010–1052)
1. **Function:** Comparative interpretation across the six competing optimization algorithms — explains the observed ranking (POA > STO > SOA > GOA > PSO... performance order) via each algorithm's known properties.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Table 1/Fig. 4 ranking of the five XGB-hybrid models plus baseline XGB.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Sequential algorithm-by-algorithm explanation, each supported by citations to the algorithm's original/review literature (Premkumar and Santhosh 2024; Trojovský et al. 2022; Al-Sarray et al. 2024; Dehghani and Trojovský 2022; Sindi et al. 2024; Rini et al. 2011; Juneja and Nagar 2016; Mirjalili et al. 2018; Zhao et al. 2019) — each citation supports *why* that specific algorithm's mathematical properties (exploration/exploitation balance, convergence speed, avoidance of local minima) produced its observed rank.
4. **Literature use:** Extensive supporting citations, one or more per algorithm, all used to *explain* (not to compare/contrast against) the paper's own ranking — i.e., literature deployed to justify a mechanism for an already-observed result, not to check the result against external benchmarks.
5. **Certainty level:** Presented with moderate confidence markers ("This may lead to slow convergence" for GOA — a hedge; but "STO algorithm maintains a good equilibrium... which improves model performance" — stated more directly) — certainty varies per claim, generally moderate/plausible-mechanism level, appropriately hedged where the mechanism (why the ranking is this precise order) is less directly testable from this paper's own data alone.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Implicitly concordant — each algorithm's observed performance is explained as consistent with its known theoretical properties from the optimization literature, no contradictions surfaced.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Practical implication drawn explicitly: "these models best suit adaptation for real-time applications because fast updates are necessary" — ties model-speed characteristics to an operational/practical recommendation.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic, comparative among the paper's own model variants (not against external studies yet).

#### Paragraph 5 (lines 1053–1072)
1. **Function:** External benchmarking — explicit comparison of this paper's accuracy against three other published soil-salinity ML studies elsewhere in the world.
2. **Finding it starts from:** This paper's best R² (0.968) vs. three cited external results.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct numeric concordance/superiority statements: "The model accuracy in Wang and Sun's 2024 study was **lower than** the models in our study" (R²=0.84 vs. 0.968); "It was **less accurate than** in our study" (Aksoy et al. 2024, R²=0.83); a third comparison to Elshewy et al. (2024, R²=0.86). Closing sentence: "Comparison with previous studies showed the potential of the machine learning model in this study."
4. **Literature use:** Three external studies used explicitly to *benchmark/contrast* — a clean, comparative literature deployment distinct from the explanatory citation-use of Paragraph 4.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as direct numeric fact (R² values), not hedged — appropriate since these are reported metrics being compared, not causal claims.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** All three comparisons are favorable (this study's R² higher in each case) — a fully concordant/superior pattern, used to support the paper's methodological contribution claim.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit self-congratulatory implication ("showed the potential of the machine learning model") — a fairly direct implication statement without much hedging, arguably closer to promotional framing than the more careful hedging seen elsewhere in this Discussion.
8. **Limitations:** None here.
9. **Role:** Comparative, positioning the paper's contribution against the broader field.

#### Paragraph 6 (lines 1073–1091)
1. **Function:** Returns to domain mechanism — explains *why* salinity intrusion is worsening in the study area, integrating multiple external causal claims from cited literature.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own spatial salinity pattern (coastal/riverside concentration) combined with contextual drivers not directly measured by this study (upstream dams, sea-level rise, subsidence).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Chain of attributed claims, each explicitly sourced: "Hien et al. (2023) have **emphasized** that by 2050, saltwater intrusion is likely to extend..."; "Nguyen et al. (2017) **reported** that the increasing trend... is the result of..."; "Nguyen and Takewaka (2020) have **emphasized** that the subsidence phenomenon... can reach −12.3 mm/yr, which is **one of the causes** that aggravate..." — each claim is attributed to a specific source using reporting verbs (emphasized, reported), a careful practice of NOT adopting these causal claims as the present paper's own demonstrated result, since this paper did not itself measure discharge, sea-level, or subsidence rate.
4. **Literature use:** Three external sources used to *support* an explanatory chain the paper itself did not test — literature substituting for data this study does not have, explicitly flagged as such by attribution language.
5. **Certainty level:** Each specific numeric/causal claim is attributed to its source paper ("Nguyen and Takewaka (2020) have emphasized that..."), which is the correct hedge — this paper reports what others found rather than claiming it demonstrated the mechanism itself.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Fully concordant — no external claim is challenged, all are integrated additively into one causal narrative.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Builds toward the following paragraphs' policy/adaptation-capacity discussion.
8. **Limitations:** Implicit — this paragraph is itself evidence that this study's design (spatial ML + survey) cannot directly test dam/subsidence/sea-level mechanisms; it must borrow explanatory power from other studies.
9. **Role:** Mechanistic, extending scope beyond what the paper's own data can show.

#### Paragraphs 7–9 (lines 1092–1156, adaptive capacity discussion)
1. **Function:** Interpreting the household survey (adaptive-capacity) findings, connecting quantitative findings (education levels, income instability, capital-access rates) to a broader "vulnerability" narrative and drawing explicit policy implications.
2. **Finding it starts from:** §4.4's five-capital assessment results (66% educational attainment at junior-high level or below; 72% primary income from agriculture but unstable; 8% cannot borrow capital at all).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Comparative framing ("Our results are similar to those of previous studies investigating the adaptive capacity of residential communities to natural hazards") — a concordance statement, followed by causal-chain reasoning linking low education → limited knowledge uptake → limited adaptive capacity, and unstable income → limited capital accumulation → limited adaptive capacity.
4. **Literature use:** The source text states "Our results are similar to those of previous studies investigating the adaptive capacity of residential communities to natural hazards" without naming a specific author/year at this point in the paragraph — the identity of these "previous studies" is `[KHÔNG XÁC ĐỊNH ĐƯỢC]` from this sentence alone. This is a weaker/less-specific literature deployment than Paragraphs 4–6, functioning as broad concordance support rather than a pointed, attributable comparison.
5. **Certainty level:** Moderate — causal chains stated fairly directly ("This is in part because...", "leading to difficulties in...") without heavy hedging, appropriate since these are drawn directly from the paper's own interview data (a legitimate basis for moderate-confidence claims about this specific sample, though not causally testable at a population level given the cross-sectional design).
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Concordant with general adaptive-capacity literature.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Explicit, direct policy recommendations: "it is important to emphasize the role and effectiveness of policies of local governments..."; "Lending policies... need to cover all households while improving the efficiency of capital use" — clearly prescriptive/practical implications, appropriately following from (not overreaching beyond) the survey findings about capital-access gaps.
8. **Limitations:** Named directly: "to apply this theoretical framework in different regions, it is necessary to use factors specifically pertinent to each region... data collection in any region is difficult" — a generalizability limitation, interleaved mid-Discussion rather than saved for the end.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive + practical implications, this paper's primary "so what for policy" payload.

#### Paragraphs 10–12 (lines 1157–1220, closing)
1. **Function:** Named methodological limitation (ML extrapolation problem), synthesis paragraph, and forward-looking closing statement.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own modeling approach (single-region-trained ML models).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct limitation statement: "A significant problem when using machine learning is that of extrapolation. Each model built is adapted only to one set of data... evaluating the soil salinity in other regions is challenging." Followed by proposed mitigations (citing Tran and Kim 2022) — again a limitation-then-mitigation pattern rather than an unresolved caveat.
4. **Literature use:** Tran and Kim (2022) cited to support a proposed solution direction (combining ML with hydrodynamic/RS models).
5. **Certainty level:** Directly stated as a known, general ML limitation (not hedged) — appropriately confident since this is a well-established property of trained statistical models, not a speculative claim.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The paper closes by re-asserting practical utility ("a solid scientific basis for developing policies") — closing-loop return to the introduction's stated goals.
8. **Limitations:** A second, explicit limitation is named later (lines 1201–1209): only 62 soil samples, no time-series capability — self-critical and specific about sample-size constraints on what conclusions the salinity map can support.
9. **Role:** Limitations + closing synthesis — note the limitations paragraph appears BEFORE the final forward-looking paragraph, not as the literal last words of the Discussion (see Step 4).

### PAPER 4 — Chounna Yemele et al. 2026, `§5 Discussion` (standalone)

#### §5.1 "Observed rates of vertical land motion" — Paragraph 1 (lines 777–796)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting — restates the headline quantitative finding, then immediately validates it against two external studies.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Mean VLM rate ≈ −3.0 mm/yr (Fig. 5), 97.9% of study area subsiding.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct comparative concordance: "A comparable value was associated with Douala based on the results obtained from a global study by Davydzenka et al. (2024)..."; "Our results also **align with** the findings of Cian et al. (2019), who used InSAR to map land subsidence in 18 African coastal cities and determined that 17 out of 18 experienced subsidence, including Douala." Two independent prior studies are cited, both supporting/validating (not contrasting) the paper's own measurement.
4. **Literature use:** Davydzenka et al. (2024) and Cian et al. (2019) deployed as *validating* comparators — critically, both are lower-resolution/global-scale prior studies that only indirectly indicated Douala subsidence; this paper's local InSAR measurement is framed as confirming and refining those coarser prior signals.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as directly established fact (measured InSAR rates), high confidence — appropriate given InSAR is a direct physical measurement, not an inferred/modeled quantity.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant — no discordance to resolve, this paragraph exists specifically to demonstrate agreement with (and improvement upon) prior coarse-scale evidence.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Direct: "This significant rate poses substantial threats to the region, exacerbating the impacts of sea-level rise" — a hazard-severity implication drawn immediately from the concordant measurement.
8. **Limitations:** None yet.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting + comparative validation.

#### §5.1 — Paragraph 2 (lines 796–830)
1. **Function:** Extends the comparison geographically (regional context: other Gulf of Guinea cities) and draws a consequence-severity inference (building collapse risk) by analogy to a comparator city (Lagos).
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's subsidence measurement, now generalized regionally.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Listing concordant regional evidence (Ghana, Nigeria, Senegal studies) to build a pattern-level claim ("This suggests that most urban cities along the Gulf of Guinea are undergoing subsidence"), then an explicit analogical inference: "The occurrence of inundation and building collapse that has been observed in Douala (Tchamba and Bikoko, 2016) **may be attributed to** land subsidence, **as reported in** Nigeria by Ohenhen and Shirzaei (2022), who revealed that approximately 4000 buildings are exposed to..." — the hedge "may be attributed to" is important: the paper does NOT claim it demonstrated that Douala's building collapses were caused by subsidence (it did not study building damage directly); it proposes this as a plausible mechanism supported by analogy to a nearby, structurally similar case.
4. **Literature use:** Avornyo et al. (2023), Brempong et al. (2023), Ohenhen and Shirzaei (2022), Ikuemonisan et al. (2023), Cisse et al. (2024) — a regional literature survey used to build a pattern-of-evidence argument, then one specific study (Ohenhen and Shirzaei 2022, Lagos) used analogically to propose an untested local mechanism.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly hedged ("may be attributed to") — correctly flagged as a plausible but untested mechanism, since this paper's own design (InSAR + LULC) cannot establish a causal link to building collapse events without structural/damage data.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Concordant regional pattern-building.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** "necessitating urgent attention and intervention strategies" — direct call-to-action implication following the hazard-severity claim.
8. **Limitations:** None explicit yet, though the "may be attributed to" hedge functions as an implicit limitation marker.
9. **Role:** Comparative, extending severity implications by analogy.

#### §5.1 — Paragraph 3 (lines 831–843)
1. **Function:** Global contextualization — situates Douala's rate against world-famous subsidence cities (Jakarta, Venice) to calibrate relative severity.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Douala's ~3 mm/yr rate.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explicit magnitude comparison: "the current coastland subsidence in Douala... is **significantly smaller than** those experienced by other cities, such as Jakarta [~10 cm/yr]. **However**, the rate is **comparable to** the sea-level rise." — a two-part comparative move: first downplaying severity relative to an extreme case, then re-escalating severity by comparing against a different, more locally relevant benchmark (regional sea-level rise rate).
4. **Literature use:** Taftazani et al. (2022) for Jakarta, Teatini et al. (2005) for Venice — used for magnitude calibration/contrast, not mechanism support.
5. **Certainty level:** Numeric facts stated directly.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** A deliberate mixed framing — "smaller than X, but comparable to Y" — avoids both under- and over-stating the finding's significance, a notably balanced rhetorical move.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** "This is particularly significant given the low elevation of Douala above sea level" — ties the magnitude comparison back to the paper's own elevation data (§4.1), reinforcing why even a "moderate" rate matters here specifically.
8. **Limitations:** None yet.
9. **Role:** Comparative, calibrating severity.

#### §5.1 — Paragraph 4 (lines 844–860)
1. **Function:** Explicit limitations/uncertainty paragraph, addressing an anomalous sub-finding (the 2.1% uplift points) with multiple candidate explanations, one flagged as an artifact.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The temporal-snapshot nature of the study and the unexplained uplift signal.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct scope-limitation statement: "this study offers a **snapshot** of current VLM rates, which **could change rapidly**..." Then for the uplift anomaly: two competing explanations are offered without resolving between them — "Positive values of VLM **can result from** the effect of unaccounted horizontal motions... **Another potential explanation is that** the uplift results from sediment accumulation... **Further investigations are needed to understand why**..." This is a clean example of the paper explicitly declining to overclaim: it presents a genuine anomaly, offers two plausible mechanisms (one methodological artifact, one physical), and explicitly defers resolution to future work rather than picking one.
4. **Literature use:** Gambolati and Teatini (2015, 2021), Minderhoud et al. (2020) support the general point that subsidence rates are non-stationary and can accelerate under groundwater exploitation.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly low/undetermined — "can result from," "another potential explanation," "further investigations are needed" — this is the clearest example across all four papers of a paragraph that ends in genuine unresolved uncertainty rather than being resolved or hedged-but-committed.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A — this is an internal anomaly, not a literature comparison.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** "further detailed investigations are required to elucidate the factors causing land subsidence... mitigate its occurrence, predict and prevent larger magnitudes in the future" — implication is a research-agenda call, not a practice recommendation.
8. **Limitations bounding:** This paragraph is the clearest "explicitly flagged as untested" certainty-level paragraph in the whole four-paper corpus.
9. **Role:** Limitations/uncertainty, interleaved within §5.1 rather than deferred to a final subsection.

#### §5.2 "Land use land cover (LULC) change" — Paragraph (lines 871–903)
1. **Function:** Interpreting the LULC transition results, cross-validating against an independent prior local study.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The five-fold increase in built-up area, and the fine-grained transition-matrix results (Tables 3–5).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct comparative correlation statement: "The expansion of the anthropogenic footprint **correlates with** the study by Nfotabong-Atheull et al. (2013), which revealed that 39.9% of mangrove forests disappeared... from 1974 to 2003..." — numeric cross-validation against an independent, differently-scoped prior dataset (different time window, different specific metric — mangrove loss vs. this paper's tree-cover/built-up-area transition) used as convergent, not identical, supporting evidence.
4. **Literature use:** Nfotabong-Atheull et al. (2013) deployed as an independent corroborating data source with overlapping but non-identical scope — a "converging lines of evidence" use rather than direct replication.
5. **Certainty level:** Presented as established fact; the word "correlates" is used loosely as "is consistent with," not a formal statistical correlation claim in this sentence.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Concordant.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes the LULC finding is not an artifact of this study's specific data source, since prior independent work shows the same directional trend.
8. **Limitations:** None here.
9. **Role:** Comparative/validating.

#### §5.3 "Land-use-driven land subsidence" — Paragraph 1 (lines 909–931)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — explains the central finding (inverse relationship between subsidence rate and time-since-urbanization) via a specific physical mechanism.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Table 6/Fig. 10 result: areas urbanized 1992–2022 (long-standing built-up) show lowest subsidence (1.9 mm/yr); areas urbanized 2017–2022 (recent, especially ex-tree-cover) show highest (7.1 mm/yr).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct mechanistic attribution: "This trend **can be attributed to the fact that** recently urbanised areas are still undergoing consolidation due to the recent loading increase exerted by structures on the land surface." Followed by an additional contributing mechanism, hedged: "Moreover, tree root degradation during land-use change **potentially** contribute to land subsidence (Huning et al., 2024)."
4. **Literature use:** Galloway and Burbey (2011), Gambolati and Teatini (2021) support the general anthropogenic-subsidence-driver framework; Huning et al. (2024) supports the more speculative secondary mechanism (tree-root degradation), appropriately hedged with "potentially."
5. **Certainty level:** Two-tiered — the primary mechanism (consolidation under new structural load) is stated with higher confidence (established geotechnical principle, well-supported by the clean inverse time-relationship in the data); the secondary mechanism (root degradation) is explicitly hedged ("potentially contribute").
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A (own-data mechanistic interpretation); the finding matches the general geotechnical-consolidation theory (implicit concordance).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Closes with a direct practical implication: "an appropriate land-use management mechanism must be considered as an adaptive or mitigating measure for controlling land subsidence."
8. **Limitations:** None yet in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic.

#### §5.3 — Paragraph 2 (lines 932–955)
1. **Function:** Interpreting the building-height/subsidence-rate relationship, cross-validated against multiple independent prior studies, followed by an explicit methodological limitation with a forward-looking mitigation path.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Fig. 11 — subsidence rate decreases with increasing building height, in both residential and non-residential zones.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Mechanistic hedge: "Taller buildings experienced less subsidence, **potentially because of** their deep foundations, which made them more stable and unaffected by shallow soil compaction..." Then a concordance chain: "De Wit et al. (2021) reported **similar observations** in the Mekong Delta, as did Koster et al. (2018), Verberne et al. (2023), and Maoret et al. (2024) in cities in The Netherlands." Multiple independent geographic contexts cited as converging evidence for the same structural mechanism.
4. **Literature use:** Four independent studies (different deltas/countries) used as convergent, cross-regional supporting evidence — arguably the strongest literature-triangulation move seen across all four papers, deploying geographically diverse studies to argue the mechanism (deep foundations bypass shallow compressible layers) is general, not site-specific.
5. **Certainty level:** Hedged ("potentially because of") for the causal mechanism itself, but treated as reasonably well-supported given the cross-regional concordance.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Fully concordant across four external comparators.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Deferred — this paragraph pivots directly into a methodological limitation rather than a practice implication.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** Explicit and detailed: "we acknowledge that the ability to distinguish deformation patterns between tall and low-rise buildings **could be improved using** a Persistent Scatterer InSAR (PS-InSAR) processing chain. The InSAR dataset employed in this study provides **first-order insights**... the processing approach used **introduces spatial smoothing, which may obscure** localized variations..." — a precise, method-specific limitation (spatial resolution/smoothing trade-off) tied directly back to the processing choices described in §3.2 (Methods), demonstrating close linkage between Methods and Discussion limitation language.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive + explicit limitations, interleaved (not deferred to end).

#### §5.3 — Paragraph 3 (lines 956–977)
1. **Function:** Addressing an un-modeled causal factor (groundwater extraction) with an honest data-availability limitation and a partial, coarser-resolution substitute line of evidence.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own acknowledgment that groundwater pumping is a plausible major driver but was not directly measured.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct data-gap admission: "groundwater extraction data are **scarce** for the study area, **limiting our possibility to quantify** aquifer depletion and its contribution to land subsidence." Then a partial substitute is offered: GRACE satellite data showing declining terrestrial water storage — immediately qualified: "the GRACE data have a **coarse spatial resolution** (~300–400 km), which **constrains their capacity to resolve** localized groundwater variations... it **does not capture** the fine-scale dynamics necessary to establish a direct spatial correspondence."
4. **Literature use:** GRACE mission data used as the best-available (but explicitly insufficient) substitute evidence.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly and carefully bounded — the paper states what GRACE *can* show (regional trend) versus what it *cannot* show (local correspondence to the measured subsidence pattern), a precise, non-overclaiming use of imperfect secondary evidence.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** "This calls for the need to establish in-situ monitoring network for data collection" — a specific infrastructure/monitoring recommendation directly motivated by the identified data gap.
