8. **Limitations bounding:** This paragraph is essentially built entirely around bounding what can and cannot be claimed given data scarcity — a model example of transparent epistemic-uncertainty handling.
9. **Role:** Limitations, immediately preceding the Conclusion — this is the closest any of the four papers comes to a "limitations right before the end" placement, though even here it is topically embedded (about one specific unmeasured driver) rather than a generic caveats list.

### PAPER 5 — Li et al. 2021, `§4 Results and discussion` (merged — no standalone Discussion)

Structurally identical to Shahi et al.: no Discussion section boundary exists. Interpretation is threaded through five result-reporting subsections (§4.1–§4.5). The paragraphs below are drawn from the interpretive portions of each.

#### §4.1 "Validation of the results" — interpretive paragraph (lines 546–563)
1. **Function:** Reports the primary validation statistic (match ratio against independent InSAR data) and immediately qualifies an apparent partial failure case rather than letting the headline number stand unexamined.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Total match ratio of 85% between FWBM hazard classification and the sign of InSAR-observed subsidence-rate change (Table 2).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** After stating the match ratio as fact, the paragraph pivots on "Notably, Table 1 showed that some points with SrD [subsidence rate decrease] are located in the high-hazard area" — naming the imperfect fraction of the validation directly rather than reporting only the 85% headline. The explanation that follows ("In fact, there are portions of the study plain where, because the piezometric level did not recover significantly, land subsidence rates remained larger than 50 mm/yr in 2017... although smaller than the values observed in previous years") reframes the apparent mismatch as an artifact of the binary SrD/SrI validation scheme (which only tests direction of rate *change*, not absolute hazard level) rather than a genuine model failure.
4. **Literature use:** None in this paragraph — self-contained reasoning about the validation design's own limitation.
5. **Certainty level:** The 85% figure is stated as fact; the explanation for the residual mismatch is presented with moderate confidence, grounded in the paper's own domain knowledge of the site (citing the authors' own prior work, Zhu et al. 2020a, b) rather than as fully demonstrated.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Handles a partial/imperfect validation result directly — names the specific subset of points where the sign-based validation disagrees with the classified hazard level, rather than reporting only the aggregate success rate.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — sets up §4.2's comparison against the non-fuzzy WBM ablation as the next step in establishing the model's value.
8. **Limitations:** Implicitly, this paragraph reveals a limitation of the validation *method itself* (rate-change sign only, not absolute-level validation) without naming it explicitly as such.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/validating, opening the interpretive sequence.

##### §4.2 "Effect of fuzziness on model results" — interpretive paragraph (lines 612–629)
1. **Function:** Ablation-comparison paragraph — argues for the fuzzy-weighting design choice by demonstrating where the non-fuzzy alternative (WBM) fails directionally.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Fuzzy vs. non-fuzzy weight table (Table 2) and the spatial difference map (Fig. 7a) between FWBM and WBM hazard classifications.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct causal/explanatory statement, not heavily hedged: "We found that the stronger the semantic fuzziness of the factor importance is, the greater the uncertainty of factor weights is, which may make the greater difference in hazard probability results" (one hedge, "may"). Then a concrete, location-specific demonstration: "the WBM overestimates the subsidence hazard level for area 1... and partially the level for area 2... where the subsidence rate decreased in recent years. In addition, the WBM underestimates the hazard level for area 3... where the subsidence rate increased" — a paired over-/under-estimation pattern used as the evidentiary basis for preferring FWBM.
4. **Literature use:** None — this is a within-study ablation comparison (own model vs. own simplified alternative), not a literature comparison.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated with moderate-to-high confidence; grounded directly in the paper's own comparison data (Table 1's total match ratio: FWBM 85% vs. WBM 82%), an appropriate basis for a within-study methodological claim.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A (internal ablation, not external comparison).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit methodological implication: fuzzy weighting is worth the added complexity because it corrects specific, spatially identifiable directional errors, not just a marginal aggregate-accuracy gain.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, comparative (against an internal ablation baseline rather than external literature) — a paper-specific variant of the "benchmark against a named alternative" move seen with external comparators in other papers.

#### §4.3 "Effect of assessment factors on hazard probability" — interpretive paragraph (lines 632–660)
1. **Function:** Concordance-first validation of the headline mechanism (higher groundwater-decline rate → higher hazard), followed by mechanistic explanation of a non-monotonic secondary pattern.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Per-factor hazard-probability curves (Fig. 8): confined/unconfined aquifer decline rate >1 m/yr yields max probabilities 0.65/0.68; compressible-sediment thickness 160–170 m yields 0.32; Quaternary thickness 400–500 m yields 0.35, both then *decreasing* at greater thickness.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct concordance statement: "the higher the reduction rate of the groundwater levels is, the higher the hazard probability is, which **is consistent with** the general understanding and with the outcome of previous studies" (Tomás et al. 2010; Galloway and Burbey 2011; Zhu et al. 2015) — validation-first. The paper then addresses the counter-intuitive decrease in hazard probability at the *largest* compressible-sediment/Quaternary thicknesses with an explicit two-factor mechanism: "an aquifer system with thick compressible sediments is more susceptible to causing large land subsidence, **but this takes place only if** a certain drawdown of the piezometric head, which is the triggering factor, occurs in that portion of the subsurface... **This can explain why** the hazard probability decreases for the largest values..." — thickness alone is reframed as a necessary-but-not-sufficient condition, resolving an apparent non-monotonicity rather than leaving it unexplained.
4. **Literature use:** Tomás et al. (2010), Galloway and Burbey (2011), Zhu et al. (2015) support the groundwater-decline concordance; Lei et al. (2016) supports the general compressible-sediment-thickness/subsidence relationship (>100 m threshold) — both concordant, supporting uses.
5. **Certainty level:** The groundwater-decline mechanism is stated with high confidence, backed by multi-source concordance; the thickness non-monotonicity explanation is presented as a logical resolution ("this can explain why") grounded in the model's own two-factor structure (thickness × triggering drawdown) rather than new external evidence — an appropriately confident but self-referential mechanistic account.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The groundwater-decline relationship is concordant; the thickness non-monotonicity is a genuinely counter-intuitive pattern within the study's own results, and the paragraph resolves it analytically rather than flagging it as unexplained — a distinct move from papers that leave an anomaly explicitly unresolved (cf. Chounna Yemele's uplift paragraph).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes the factor-specific hazard thresholds used later for spatial hazard mapping (§4.5).
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, concordance + mechanistic, resolving an internal anomaly analytically rather than deferring it.

#### §4.4 "Temporal change in the subsidence hazard" — interpretive paragraph (lines 663–701)
1. **Function:** Attributes the observed decrease in overall hazard probability over time to a specific, named policy/infrastructure intervention, then supports the attribution by analogy to an independent case elsewhere.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Hazard probability distribution decreasing from a 0.01–51.30% range (2003–2010) to 0.01–28.33% (2015–2017) (Fig. 9).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct causal attribution: "Overall, the subsidence hazard decreased. **This can be credited to** the significant exploitation of groundwater resources and the following utilization policies" — followed by a specific causal chain naming the South-to-North Water Transfer Project (SNWP-CR) as reducing aquifer pressure and slowing groundwater-level decline. The paragraph then closes with an analogical support move: "**Similarly**, the California State Water Project, which is also a large water-transfer system, has been fundamental to controlling land subsidence in the Central Valley, California (Sneed et al., 2018)" — an independent, geographically distant case used to support the plausibility of the water-transfer-project mechanism.
4. **Literature use:** Zhang et al. (2015) supports the EGRR-driven drawdown mechanism (own earlier period); Zhu et al. (2020b) supports the SNWP-CR mechanism; Sneed et al. (2018) is deployed analogically (California) to support the general causal claim (water-transfer projects can control subsidence), not to validate this study's specific numeric result.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated fairly directly ("can be credited to"), a moderate-confidence causal attribution appropriate for a policy-intervention explanation supported by a clear temporal coincidence (project start date aligning with the hazard-probability decline) plus an independent analogous case, though the paper's own design (observational, no counterfactual) cannot fully isolate the water-transfer project from other concurrent changes.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A — this is the paper's own temporal-trend interpretation, supported analogically rather than contrasted.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit policy validation — the finding functions as evidence that the SNWP-CR intervention "worked," supporting continued/similar water-management policy.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic, using cross-regional analogy as supporting (not benchmarking) evidence — a lighter-weight version of Chounna Yemele's multi-study triangulation (B6), here with a single analogous case rather than several.

#### §4.5 "Spatial distribution of subsidence hazard" — interpretive paragraph (lines 704–720)
1. **Function:** Concordance-first validation of the hazard-classification scheme itself, followed by a spatial mechanistic explanation of the hazard gradient.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Four-level hazard classification (10.7% high, 17.5% medium, 29.7% low, 42.1% very low) and the spatial location of medium–high-hazard sites (Tianzhu, Nanfaxin, Gaoliying, Houshayu).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct concordance statement first: "Four subsidence hazard levels are classified from the probability map... **consistent with previous studies** (Yang et al., 2013; Zhu et al., 2015)" — validating the classification scheme's face validity before interpreting the specific spatial pattern. The spatial mechanism is then stated plainly: "As the thickness of the compressible sediments increases from north to south, the subsidence hazard probability increases accordingly" — directly linking back to the §4.3 factor analysis.
4. **Literature use:** Yang et al. (2013), Zhu et al. (2015) — concordant, validating the classification approach generally, not this specific numeric breakdown.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established fact, consistent with the paper's own data (InSAR-measured subsidence rate up to 84.9 mm/yr in the flagged high-hazard sites) directly cross-referenced within the same paragraph — a self-consistent, high-confidence closing statement.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** Concordant.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Closing statement of the results-and-discussion section: "Overall, the area of high subsidence hazard decreases versus time due to the reduction in the rate of groundwater level change" — a direct synthesis tying together §4.3 (mechanism) and §4.4 (temporal trend) implications, functioning as the paper's practical takeaway before the formal Conclusions section restates it.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph — Li et al.'s only explicit limitations appear in the Conclusions section (prior-probability determination method, unaddressed cumulative-subsidence metric), outside the merged Results-and-discussion body; this is a structural difference from the other 8 papers, where at least one limitation is interleaved within the Discussion/Results-and-discussion body itself (flagged in Step 3).
9. **Role:** Closing/synthesizing for the merged section, concordance + mechanistic.

### PAPER 6 — Zhu et al. 2020, `§5 Discussion` (standalone)

#### §5 opening paragraph (lines 510–521)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting — frames the causal-attribution question explicitly and pre-emptively rules out two candidate explanations before the subsections build the positive case.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The two subsidence zones identified in Results (§4.2), now needing causal explanation.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explicit elimination move: "Tectonic movement and underground caves can cause ground deformation. **However**, the high displacement rates, which exceed 5 cm/yr at many locations, **are not in agreement with** the tectonic movement rates, which are estimated to be less than 0.5 cm/yr (Guo et al., 2019). In addition, there are no known underground caves..." — two alternative mechanisms are considered and rejected using a magnitude mismatch (tectonic rate too small by an order of magnitude) and an absence-of-evidence argument (no known karst features), before the paper commits to its preferred explanation (groundwater extraction + coal mining).
4. **Literature use:** Guo et al. (2019) supplies the comparator tectonic-rate figure used to rule out tectonics — a *contrastive* literature use where the citation's role is to falsify a candidate explanation, not to support the paper's own claim directly.
5. **Certainty level:** The rejection of tectonic/karst causes is stated with high confidence (quantitative mismatch, absence of known geological features); the preferred explanation ("we infer that this deformation is mainly caused by...") is explicitly flagged with "infer," a moderate-confidence hedge appropriate given this is the paper's central causal claim still to be substantiated in the following subsections.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A — this is elimination of rival hypotheses, not a comparison of the paper's own finding against prior work on the same mechanism.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up the rest of the Discussion: "we infer that this deformation is mainly caused by extensive extraction of groundwater and coal. This will be elaborated more in the following."
8. **Limitations:** None yet.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting, but structurally a rival-hypothesis-elimination move — a paper-specific variant not seen with this explicit "consider and reject two alternatives before proceeding" structure in the original four papers.

#### §5.1 "Correlation between ground deformation and groundwater withdraw" — Paragraph 1 (lines 524–535)
1. **Function:** Establishes the general groundwater-extraction-causes-subsidence mechanism as literature-concordant background before the paper's own site-specific data is brought in.
2. **Finding it starts from:** General knowledge that groundwater has been exploited in the Heze region since the 1970s.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct, unhedged mechanistic statement ("Long-term exploitation of groundwater brings about a compaction of the fine clay layer and finally results in the ground subsidence") immediately backed by three citations: "Previous research indicates that the excessive groundwater withdrawal is one of the main reasons for ground deformation in this area (Hu et al., 2004; Guo et al., 2019; Xue et al., 2005)."
4. **Literature use:** Hu et al. (2004), Guo et al. (2019), Xue et al. (2005) — concordant, prior-established-mechanism support, all specifically about this same study area rather than general geomechanics.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established fact — appropriate since this is a well-documented, area-specific mechanism from prior local studies, not a new claim being tested here.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up the more detailed evidentiary paragraphs that follow.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Orienting/mechanistic-background, establishing the literature basis before presenting new own-data evidence.

#### §5.1 — Paragraph 2 (lines 553–572)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — links the paper's own time-series InSAR evidence to groundwater decline, then explains the rural/urban asymmetry via depth-of-extraction differences.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Time-series displacement at 11 rural/urban points (Fig. 6) and shallow/deep groundwater-level records (Fig. 7a, b).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Inferential chaining from displacement pattern to cause: "It suggests that the groundwater is still excessively exploited and the groundwater recharge is insufficient to supply the exploitation." Then an explicit urban-vs-rural asymmetry explanation: "One reason for the large settlement in urban areas is that large quantities of the deep groundwater (over 200 m depth) are extracted to meet industrial needs, while shallow groundwater is extracted for domestic use" — supported by quantified groundwater-level-decline numbers (shallow: 0.5 m in one year; deep: ~6 m in ~2 years) and an external quantitative citation (Xu et al. 2017: >1 billion tons over-extracted annually).
4. **Literature use:** Xu et al. (2017), Shandong Provincial Bureau of Statistics (2020), Feng et al. (2015), Yue (2020) — all supply quantitative context (extraction volumes, well counts, historical decline rates) supporting the mechanistic narrative, a data-substitution use similar to Nguyen et al. 2025's borrowed-mechanism pattern (A3) but here citing statistical/administrative sources rather than peer-reviewed mechanism studies.
5. **Certainty level:** The urban/rural depth-extraction asymmetry is presented as "one reason," an explicit hedge signaling this is offered as the most plausible explanation rather than an exhaustively tested one.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A (own-data interpretation).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes the depth-specific extraction mechanism used in the following paragraphs' precipitation-correlation analysis.
8. **Limitations:** An explicit data-access limitation appears here: "As the aquifer's detailed information is not public, we cannot obtain enough information to investigate the detailed relationship between the subsidence and the groundwater level" — stated directly, mid-paragraph, immediately followed by a workaround ("switch to investigate the relationship between the subsidence and the precipitation") rather than left as a dead end.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic with an interleaved limitation-and-workaround move.

#### §5.1 — Paragraph 3 (lines 582–623)
1. **Function:** Mechanistic elaboration using seasonal precipitation as a proxy for shallow-groundwater recharge, culminating in a lagged-correlation finding for rural areas and a proposed causal chain.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Monthly precipitation record (Fig. 7c) and the residual (detrended) rural displacement time series (Fig. 7d).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** A detailed seasonal-cycle narrative (dry-season stability → spring drawdown from irrigation → summer rebound from rain) is built first as background, then the paper explicitly proposes and immediately hedges its causal interpretation: "we **may hypothesize** that the ground subsidence is caused by both the hydraulic head change and the seasonal groundwater variations. Moreover, the time lag is induced by..." — "may hypothesize" is an explicit plausible-but-not-fully-tested marker, appropriately calibrated because the paper lacks direct local aquifer data (per the prior paragraph's stated limitation) and is inferring the mechanism indirectly through precipitation as a proxy.
4. **Literature use:** Jia (2015), Ma and Feng (2014), Yu et al. (2001) support the claim that precipitation is the dominant shallow-groundwater recharge source (85% of supply) — literature substituting for the unavailable direct aquifer data, an explicit workaround for the limitation named in the previous paragraph.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly hedged ("may hypothesize") — a clear example of a paper flagging a plausible-but-untested mechanism using its own stated language, appropriate given the indirect (precipitation-as-proxy) nature of the evidence.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None yet — sets up the following urban-area contrast paragraph.
8. **Limitations:** Continues the data-substitution workaround from the previous paragraph.
9. **Role:** Mechanistic, explicitly hedged hypothesis-generation.

#### §5.1 — Paragraph 4 (lines 624–640)
1. **Function:** Discordant-pattern handling — the urban-area precipitation-displacement relationship does NOT show the rural pattern, and the paragraph explains why rather than ignoring the mismatch, then closes with an external concordant validation for the general subsidence-groundwater link.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The rural-area seasonal correlation (previous paragraph) fails to appear in urban-area displacement (Fig. 7e).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct discordance statement: "In contrast, Fig. 7e shows that there is no straightforward relationship between the precipitation and the variations of displacement in urban area." Explained via the same depth-of-extraction mechanism established earlier: "It **may be due to** the extraction of deep rather than shallow groundwater, as the deep groundwater is difficult to recharge..." The paragraph then closes with an external concordance citation: "H. Zhang et al. (2018) found that the subsidence has a significant correlation (up to 0.96) with the decline of deep groundwater level in another city near Heze" — used to support the deep-groundwater mechanism analogically, since this paper itself lacks the deep-aquifer data to demonstrate the correlation directly for Heze.
4. **Literature use:** Qiao and Lin (2006), Zhang (2013) support the deep-aquifer recharge-difficulty claim; H. Zhang et al. (2018) supplies an external, quantified (r=0.96) analogous validation from a nearby city — deployed because the paper's own data cannot directly test the deep-groundwater/subsidence correlation.
5. **Certainty level:** Hedged ("may be due to") for the urban-specific mechanism; the external validation (Zhang et al. 2018) is reported as established fact from that other study, correctly attributed rather than adopted as this paper's own demonstrated result.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** This is the clearest discordant-finding-handling paragraph in this paper — a expected pattern (precipitation correlation) explicitly fails in one subgroup (urban) and the paper explains the failure via a mechanism (extraction depth) rather than omitting or downplaying the null result.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces that urban subsidence is driven by deep, not shallow, groundwater dynamics, which matters for later mitigation-planning implications.
8. **Limitations:** None new here.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, discordance-handling + externally validated mechanism.

#### §5.2 "Correlation between ground deformation and underground mining" — Paragraph 1 (lines 661–677)
1. **Function:** Discordant/unexpected-pattern handling — the mining-area displacement pattern does not match the expected signature of direct mining-induced subsidence, and the paragraph uses this mismatch to propose an indirect mechanism instead.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Time-series displacement at four mining-area points (M1–M4, Fig. 8): linear trend, cumulative subsidence not exceeding 50 cm over 4 years.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explicit expectation-violation statement: "In fact, the mining-induced subsidence is generally nonlinear with high rates, which can reach up to several meters in a short time... These characteristics indicate that the subsidence near M1–M4 locations **may not be** directly induced by underground mining." The paper then proposes an indirect two-part mechanism (groundwater outflow through mining-induced fractures; fault reactivation from mining), each independently referenced.
4. **Literature use:** Rapantova et al. (2007), Xu et al. (2018) support the fracture-network groundwater-drainage mechanism; Islam and Shinjo (2009), Wang et al. (2016), Y. H. Zhang et al. (2018) support the fault-reactivation mechanism — both literature uses are of the "established mechanism from elsewhere, applied to explain a new instance the paper's own data cannot directly confirm" type (A3-pattern).
5. **Certainty level:** The observed pattern-mismatch (linear, low-magnitude vs. expected nonlinear, high-magnitude) is stated as established fact from the data; the proposed indirect mechanism is explicitly speculative in the following paragraph's language ("a plausible explanation of this deformation... **could be**").
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** A clean example — the naive expectation (subsidence directly above mining = mining-caused) is explicitly falsified by the data's own kinematic signature, and the paper does not force the naive interpretation onto the data.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Deferred to the following paragraphs (fringe-pattern analysis) and to the Conclusions' call for continued monitoring.
8. **Limitations:** None yet.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, discordance-handling.

#### §5.2 — Paragraph 2 (lines 678–716)
1. **Function:** Visual/interferometric evidence paragraph presenting a genuinely unresolved secondary anomaly, explicitly deferred rather than resolved, followed by a second site's confirmation and a practical closing implication.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Interferogram fringe patterns (Figs. 9, 10) showing "primary" subsidence basins (directly above mining, dense fringes) and "secondary" basins (offset 3–7 km, sparser fringes, arc-shaped in one case).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** For the first site (Fig. 9/box a): "A **plausible explanation** of this deformation near M1 and M2 locations **could be** the groundwater outflow to working panels and its drainage, which finally triggers the ground subsidence" — explicitly flagged as one plausible account, not a demonstrated mechanism. For the second site (Fig. 10/box b), an additional complicating observation is added: "The arc-shaped subsidence **does not agree with** the law of the ground movement induced by underground mining. Figure 3 shows the arc-shaped subsidence is consistent with the fault, which implies... **Unfortunately, it is difficult to** investigate the interactions among the underground mining, fault activation and ground deformation, since we do not have detailed information, such as the distribution of unknown faults and abandoned mines..." — an explicit, named data-availability limitation (unknown fault/mine locations) presented as the reason full mechanistic resolution is not possible here.
4. **Literature use:** He et al. (1994), Litwiniszyn (1956), Zhu et al. (2020) support the general bowl-shaped mining-subsidence signature used as the comparison baseline against which the "primary" basins are validated as normal and the "secondary" basins are flagged as anomalous.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly low/deferred — "plausible explanation... could be," "Unfortunately, it is difficult to investigate," "It will be interesting to further investigate the cause of secondary subsidence when more data become available" (this last sentence recurs verbatim in the Conclusions). This is the clearest genuinely-unresolved-anomaly paragraph in this paper, structurally parallel to Chounna Yemele's uplift-anomaly paragraph in the original four-paper set.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The primary basins are concordant with established mining-subsidence signatures; the secondary basins are discordant, and the paper explicitly declines to force a resolution, offering two candidate mechanisms without picking between them.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Closes with a practical, safety-oriented implication rather than a research-agenda-only statement: "the concomitant ground deformation (i.e., secondary subsidence basin) induced by underground mining suggests that coal extraction must be carried out very carefully, especially when there are widespread faults" — notable because the practical implication is drawn even though the underlying mechanism is explicitly unresolved, on the basis that *some* mining-related cause is well-supported even if the exact pathway is not.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The named data gap (unknown fault/mine locations) is the explicit basis for why the mechanism cannot be fully resolved — precisely scoped to what is and is not knowable from available data.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, genuinely-unresolved-anomaly handling, closing with a scoped practical implication despite persisting uncertainty — this paragraph is also reproduced almost verbatim as a bullet point in the Conclusions section, indicating the authors treat it as a key takeaway rather than a peripheral caveat.

### PAPER 7 — Nguyen (Nghia) et al. 2024/2025, `§4 Discussion` (standalone)

#### Paragraph 1 (lines 976–1010)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting-and-synthesizing — consolidates the scenario-by-scenario Results into a ranked driver-importance narrative before any new interpretation begins.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The full set of single- and combined-driver scenario results (S0–S7) from §3.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Aggregative restatement similar in structure to Yuen's opening synthesis paragraph (B1-type move) — ranks drivers from least to most impactful ("riverbed lowering... and storm surges... are not expected to substantially impact... However, the results emphasizes that land subsidence and tidal influence combined with SLR will be the primary factors..."), then restates the two headline combined-scenario numbers (96% of CMP inundated by 2050 under S6; 99% by 2100 under S7) before an explicit scenario-class caveat: "these projections are based on the SSP5-8.5 scenarios, which represent the low likelihood, high impact situations" — the emissions-scenario caveat is placed immediately after the headline numbers, not deferred to a separate limitations paragraph.
4. **Literature use:** None in this paragraph — pure synthesis of the paper's own scenario results.
5. **Certainty level:** The ranking of driver importance is stated as directly demonstrated (own model output); the SSP5-8.5 caveat explicitly bounds the certainty of the specific percentage figures as scenario-conditional, not unconditional predictions.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A — synthesis, not comparison.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up the remainder of the Discussion: "The ramifications of this increasing trend in inundation are now deliberated in relation to the region's future sustainability and its capacity to adapt."
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** The SSP5-8.5/low-likelihood-high-impact caveat is a scope-bounding statement placed at the point of maximum salience (immediately after the two headline numbers), rather than saved for a dedicated limitations paragraph later — an early instance of interleaved uncertainty-bounding in this paper.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting, synthesizing before interpreting — structurally close to Yuen §4.1 Para 1's aggregative-restatement opening.

#### Paragraph 2 (lines 1011–1028)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — extends the land-subsidence/SLR inundation finding into a specific ecological consequence (mangrove-forest loss) via a compounding, two-driver mechanism.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The land-subsidence/SLR-driven inundation increase established in Paragraph 1.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct threat statement ("poses a considerable threat of saltwater intrusion") followed by a compounding-mechanism claim: existing anthropogenic mangrove degradation (cited) is "**further compounded by** the potential rise in inundation levels driven by land subsidence and SLR. This combination of factors **threatens to accelerate** the loss of these critical ecosystems" — an additive/compounding framing (two independently-caused pressures reinforcing each other) rather than a single-cause mechanism.
4. **Literature use:** Son et al. (2015), Hauser et al. (2020), Thuy et al. (2023) support the pre-existing mangrove-degradation trend (concordant background); Barbier et al. (2008), Temmerman et al. (2013), Jones et al. (2020), Sunkur et al. (2023) support the general ecosystem-service-loss consequences of mangrove decline — all supporting citations extending the claim beyond what this paper's own hydrodynamic model directly measured (the model outputs water levels, not ecosystem/mangrove response), an A3-type attributed-extension move.
5. **Certainty level:** Moderately hedged ("threatens to accelerate," "poses a considerable threat") — appropriately calibrated since the mangrove-ecosystem consequence is not itself modeled by this paper's hydrodynamic simulation; it is an inference drawn from combining this paper's inundation results with external ecological literature.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — sets up the ecological-stakes framing that motivates the mitigation-strategy paragraph later in the Discussion.
8. **Limitations:** None explicit yet, though the reliance on external ecological literature (rather than this paper's own data) to support the ecosystem claim functions as an implicit scope marker.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, extending scope beyond what the paper's own model directly shows (A3-type).

#### Paragraph 3 (lines 1029–1038)
1. **Function:** Discordant/complicating-factor handling — introduces a potential natural compensatory process (sediment deposition offsetting subsidence-driven elevation loss) that could partially counter the paper's own headline finding, then explains why this compensatory process is itself constrained.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own land-subsidence/SLR inundation projections (Paragraph 1).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explicit concession-then-qualification structure: "Although land subsidence and SLR contribute to elevation loss and increased inundation, they **may also create** conditions favourable for sediment deposition in flooded areas. This sediment trapping mechanism **has the potential to** partially offset elevation loss... **However, the effectiveness of this natural compensatory process is significantly constrained by** the sharp reduction in sediment flow from upstream rivers, largely due to damming" — the paper raises a factor that could weaken its own central claim, then immediately shows why that factor is unlikely to matter much in practice (quantified: 57% decrease in suspended sediment flux, from 99 to 43 Mt/yr, citing Bussi et al. 2021), closing the loop rather than leaving the complication unresolved.
4. **Literature use:** Kondolf et al. (2014a) and Bussi et al. (2021) support the sediment-flux-reduction claim that constrains the compensatory mechanism; Anthony et al. (2015), Tu et al. (2019) support the resulting coastal-erosion consequence — all used to *neutralize* a potential counter-argument to the paper's own central finding rather than to extend or validate it.
5. **Certainty level:** The compensatory mechanism itself is hedged ("may also create," "has the potential to") as plausible-but-not-directly-modeled; the sediment-flux-reduction constraint on that mechanism is stated with a specific quantitative citation, giving it higher evidentiary weight than the compensatory mechanism it is countering.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** This paragraph pre-emptively addresses what could be read as a discordant/complicating consideration against the paper's own thesis, and resolves it in favor of the original claim rather than leaving genuine ambiguity — a distinctive "steelman-then-rebut" move not seen with this explicit structure in the original four papers.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces that the subsidence/SLR-driven inundation threat is not likely to be self-correcting via natural sedimentation, strengthening the case for the mitigation recommendations that follow.
8. **Limitations:** None explicit — the paragraph functions to *rule out* a limitation to the paper's own claim rather than to state one.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, discordant-consideration handling via concession-then-rebuttal.

#### Paragraph 4 (lines 1039–1051)
1. **Function:** Extends the practical stakes of the central finding to a concrete, quantified infrastructure-safety consequence.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own land-subsidence projections (0.77 m by 2050, 2.34 m by 2100) combined with an external engineering design standard.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct quantitative combination: road-crest design elevation (2.2 m above mean sea level, per national standard) minus projected subsidence yields an effective crest elevation of 1.43 m (2050) and −0.14 m (2100), compared against this paper's own projected maximum water levels (1.59 m by 2050, 2.20 m by 2100) — a specific, falsifiable numeric juxtaposition rather than a vague "infrastructure at risk" statement.
4. **Literature use:** The national design-specification document (No. 912/QĐ-BGTVT) and TCVN 4054:2005 are cited as the external standard against which this paper's own projections are evaluated — a regulatory/engineering-standard citation type distinct from the mechanism- or magnitude-comparison literature uses seen elsewhere in the corpus.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as direct numeric fact/comparison, appropriately since both quantities (design elevation, projected subsidence + water level) are given precisely and the comparison is arithmetic rather than inferential.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The implication is embedded in the numbers themselves (a road-crest elevation reduced below projected water level is a direct safety/functional failure), reinforced by a closing sentence noting infrastructure development itself adds further loading pressure that exacerbates subsidence — a feedback-loop observation.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, practical/quantitative implication — a comparative-magnitude paragraph type applied to an engineering standard rather than to another study's results.

#### Paragraph 5 (lines 1052–1080)
1. **Function:** Practical/policy implication paragraph — a long, multi-part list of mitigation recommendations, each tied back to a specific driver established earlier in the Discussion.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The cumulative set of findings from Paragraphs 1–4 (subsidence/SLR as dominant drivers, ecological stakes, infrastructure risk).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** A structured sequence of prescriptive statements, each opening with an imperative/normative marker ("it is crucial to...", "is essential for...", "can significantly reduce...", "is also equally important...", "is essential for tracking..."), each addressed to a specific driver named earlier (groundwater extraction → subsidence; sediment flow → erosion/compensatory deposition; mangrove restoration → ecological buffering; monitoring infrastructure → adaptive management). This is a longer and more exhaustively itemized practical-implication paragraph than seen in any of the original four papers.
4. **Literature use:** Minderhoud et al. (2020) is cited twice to support both the groundwater-extraction-as-primary-driver claim and the resilience-benefit claim; Kondolf et al. (2014b), Lai et al. (2024), Laksitaningtyas et al. (2022) support the sediment-flushing recommendation; Barbier et al. (2008), Temmerman et al. (2013), Jones et al. (2020), Sunkur et al. (2023), De Dominicis et al. (2023) support the mangrove-restoration recommendation — each recommendation is individually literature-grounded rather than presented as unsupported opinion.
5. **Certainty level:** Recommendations are stated prescriptively/normatively rather than hedged, appropriate for a practical-implication paragraph explicitly building on findings already established with moderate-to-high confidence earlier in the Discussion.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** This paragraph IS the implication payload — the most extensive and explicitly structured practical-recommendation paragraph in the 9-paper corpus, covering groundwater management, sediment management, water-saving technology, ecosystem restoration, engineered infrastructure, community engagement, and monitoring systems in sequence.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Practical implication, comprehensive and itemized.

#### Paragraph 6 (lines 1094–1107)
1. **Function:** First explicit limitation paragraph — names the 1D-vs-2D modeling choice as a methodological constraint, then defends the choice via a specific compensating design feature.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own modeling method (1D hydrodynamic model with quasi-2D floodplain representation), previously described in Methods.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct limitation-then-defense structure: "One limitation arises from the use of a one-dimensional model, which calculates water levels in the floodplain through spatial interpolation... While this approach may be less accurate compared to a 2D model. **However**, by integrating a quasi-2D representation of the floodplains, the model improves its capability to simulate hydrodynamic behaviour more accurately, offering a reasonable estimate when compared to 2D modelling" — a limitation named plainly, then immediately bounded by explaining the specific design choice (quasi-2D floodplain compartments) that mitigates it, a "limitation-as-motivation" pattern structurally similar to Nguyen et al. 2025's dual-sensor design defense (§5 Para 3 in the original four-paper analysis).
4. **Literature use:** None in this specific sentence — self-referential to the paper's own Methods design.
5. **Certainty level:** The limitation is stated as an acknowledged trade-off, not hedged as uncertain; the mitigating claim ("offering a reasonable estimate") is moderately confident but not quantified with a specific error bound in this paragraph.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reassures the reader that the modeling choice does not undermine the paper's central findings.
8. **Limitations bounding:** This is the paragraph's central function — precisely scoping what the 1D-vs-2D trade-off does and does not compromise.
9. **Role:** Limitations, interleaved mid-Discussion (not batched at the end) — three further limitation paragraphs follow immediately (DEM accuracy/resolution artifacts, land-subsidence driver scope, long-term-vs-short-term projection scope), making this Discussion's back half almost entirely composed of sequential, topically distinct limitation paragraphs.

#### Paragraph 7 (lines 1108–1153)
1. **Function:** Second and third limitation paragraphs (DEM resolution/vintage, and land-subsidence driver scope), each following the same named-limitation-then-bounded-defense structure as Paragraph 6.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own DEM choice (2008 MONRE DEM, 5 m × 5 m resolution) and its land-subsidence-scenario scope (groundwater-extraction-driven subsidence only).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** For the DEM: "this high level of detail also introduces certain artefacts... These elevation differences are particularly pronounced in the mapping of inundation levels... it also introduces abrupt transitions in the inundation extent that may not accurately reflect real-world conditions" — followed immediately by a bounding defense ("Despite these limitations, the 5 × 5 m DEM provides valuable insights... at a resolution suitable for regional-scale assessments"). For the temporal mismatch between the 2008 DEM and 2018 driver data: the paper explicitly quantifies the gap it is defending against ("SLR in the coastal region was relatively small, with an estimated increase of less than 5 cm... land subsidence during this period was more pronounced, with a maximum of 15 cm... the variations in relative elevation from 2008 to 2018 remain negligible within the scope of this study"). For the subsidence-driver scope: "this study focuses solely on the impact of groundwater extraction, excluding other factors... However, it is important to acknowledge that reduced sediment input from upstream could exacerbate natural subsidence processes... Therefore, while this study does not account for these additional subsidence factors, the broader context of sediment deficits should be considered as a contributing factor."
4. **Literature use:** Minderhoud et al. (2019) supports the DEM-accuracy discussion generally; Tran et al. (2016) supports the specific DEM's provenance and quality; Minderhoud et al. (2017), Zoccarato et al. (2018), Karlsrud et al. (2020) support the excluded-subsidence-factors acknowledgment.
5. **Certainty level:** Each limitation is stated plainly and then quantitatively bounded (specific centimeter figures for the DEM-vintage gap) rather than left as a vague caveat — an unusually precise, numerically-scoped limitation-bounding style relative to the rest of the corpus.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit throughout — each bounded limitation functions to reassure that the paper's central conclusions are not undermined by the named constraint.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The dominant function of this entire block — three separate, precisely quantified limitations in sequence.
9. **Role:** Limitations, interleaved, immediately following Paragraph 6's limitation and immediately preceding the final limitation paragraph — this paper's Discussion devotes proportionally more space to sequential, itemized limitations than any other paper in the corpus.

#### Paragraph 8 (lines 1173–1179, closing)
1. **Function:** Final limitation paragraph plus closing/forward-looking statement, combined in one paragraph rather than separated.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own long-term (up to 2100) projection scope.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct scope limitation: "these projections... may not fully account for short-term fluctuations or temporary environmental changes, potentially leading to an overestimation of short-term impacts" — followed immediately by an explicit forward-research call: "further research that includes short-term variability is needed to provide a more comprehensive understanding of the region's vulnerability."
4. **Literature use:** None in this paragraph.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated plainly as an acknowledged scope constraint, not hedged as uncertain about whether the constraint exists.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The forward-research call functions as this Discussion's final move before the Conclusions section, replacing what in other papers is a return-to-framing/reassertion-of-contribution closing move (cf. Yuen §4.2 Para 11, Nguyen 2025 §5's closing) with a pure future-work statement instead.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The final limitation in a four-limitation sequence (modeling dimensionality, DEM resolution/vintage, subsidence-driver scope, temporal-projection scope) — the most limitation-dense closing sequence in the 9-paper corpus.
9. **Role:** Limitations + closing, fused into one paragraph rather than separated by an intervening synthesis paragraph — a structural difference from Nguyen et al. 2025 and Chounna Yemele, where at least one substantive paragraph follows the last limitation before the Discussion ends.

### PAPER 8 — Rodrigues do Amaral et al. 2023, `§5 Discussion` (standalone)

#### Paragraph 1 (lines 1331–1355)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting — frames the general stakes (coastal vulnerability), states the paper's specific contribution to a data-scarce field, then immediately and explicitly names the design limitation (short record length) that shapes everything that follows.
2. **Finding it starts from:** No specific numeric finding yet — general framing plus the paper's own contribution claim.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** General stakes statement (population/property concentration in coastal zones, citing Shen et al. 2019) followed by an explicit novelty/gap statement ("few studies exist on the interplay between compound flood drivers during an historical TY in HCMC," citing Couasnon et al. 2022; Scheiber et al. 2023) followed immediately by a direct design-limitation admission: "it is worth noting that the length of the records of observational data used in our study is relatively short, and thus, a statistically based study is not possible. **However**, our fortunate circumstance of having sensors operational before, during and after the event... has facilitated the presentation of this case study" — the limitation is named in the very first paragraph of the Discussion (not deferred), and reframed as the specific justification for the paper's chosen design (single-event case study rather than statistical compound-flood analysis).
4. **Literature use:** Shen et al. (2019) supports general coastal-vulnerability framing; Xu et al. (2022a) supports the TY-as-key-driver framing; Couasnon et al. (2022), Scheiber et al. (2023) establish the specific literature gap this paper fills.
5. **Certainty level:** The design-limitation statement is direct and unhedged (a factual constraint on what kind of study is possible, not a probabilistic claim); the "fortunate circumstance" framing signals the authors' own awareness that their evidentiary basis is opportunistic (available instrumentation) rather than a designed sampling scheme.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes the case-study framing that legitimizes single-event, non-statistical conclusions for the remainder of the Discussion.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** This is the earliest-appearing limitation statement of any paper in the 9-paper corpus — placed in the Discussion's very first paragraph rather than interleaved later or saved for the end, because the limitation (short record) is foundational to how every subsequent claim in this Discussion must be read (case-study-scoped, not population-level-statistical).
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting, with the limitation functioning as a framing device rather than a caveat on a specific claim — a novel position for a limitation statement relative to the other 8 papers.

#### Paragraph 2 (lines 1356–1394)
1. **Function:** Concordance-and-discordance benchmarking of rainfall data products, with the paper's own comparative finding validated against extensive prior literature on one specific product's known bias.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own comparison of five rainfall products against in-situ rainfall during TY Usagi — MSWEP best, ERA5 worst.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct concordance statement for the positive finding: "MSWEP showed the best capabilities... which **are in line with** the literature (Prakash, 2019; Fernandez-Alvarez et al., 2020; Tran et al., 2023)." Then an extended discordant/negative-finding elaboration for ERA5, escalating in specificity across four consecutive external studies: "This finding **is in line with** the current literature: Lavers et al. (2022) found that the largest ERA5 errors are in the tropics... Jiang et al. (2021) found that ERA5 has difficulties in accurately detecting moderate and high daily precipitation events... Rivoire et al. (2021) also found that... ERA5 and CMORPH products would agree over the mid-latitudes, **but** they disagreed over the tropics... Slocum et al. (2022) analysis showed biases in the ERA5 environmental diagnostic quantities..." — a four-source literature-triangulation sequence, each adding a distinct dimension (regional bias, precipitation-threshold detection failure, latitude-dependent disagreement, thermodynamic-field bias) to build a comprehensive, converging explanation for why ERA5 underperformed.
4. **Literature use:** Six sources total (Prakash 2019; Fernandez-Alvarez et al. 2020; Tran et al. 2023 for MSWEP; Lavers et al. 2022; Jiang et al. 2021; Rivoire et al. 2021; Slocum et al. 2022; Xu et al. 2022b for ERA5/reanalysis limitations) — this is the most literature-dense single paragraph benchmarking exercise in the 9-paper corpus, structurally similar to Chounna Yemele's B6 cross-regional-triangulation pattern but applied to validating a data-product limitation rather than a physical mechanism.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated with high confidence throughout, appropriately so given the finding (ERA5's tropical wet bias) is extensively and independently corroborated across multiple prior studies using different methods and regions.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant — both the positive (MSWEP) and negative (ERA5) findings match prior literature; no internal contradiction is being resolved here, only external validation of both directions of the paper's own comparative result.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — validates the paper's rainfall-product choice (MSWEP) for the remainder of its own analysis, and implicitly recommends against ERA5 for similar tropical applications.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, comparative/concordance-validating — the single most extensive literature-triangulation paragraph observed across all 9 papers.

#### Paragraph 3 (lines 1395–1411)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — explains a counter-intuitive seasonal pattern (increased wet-season rainfall coinciding with *lower* river levels) via a mechanistic distinction between water level and discharge.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Seasonal water-level and discharge records at two river stations, showing minimal wet-season water-level rise despite high rainfall.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Explicit puzzle-then-resolution structure: "the increased rainfall during the wet season appears to have minimal impact on water levels at the seasonal timescale. This phenomenon **is attributed to**..." followed by "a marginal rise in discharge is discernible... **Several arguments could hold**. Firstly, we see that river levels are generally lower... However, the estimated river discharge... is higher... **We propose that** this is explained by a decrease in river water level everywhere in the river such that the slope is less influenced than the local river levels by this coastal forcing" — the paper explicitly proposes its own novel explanatory chain ("we propose"), calibrated as the paper's own inferential contribution rather than an externally validated fact.
4. **Literature use:** Ngoc et al. (2014) supports the closing sentence about upstream dam-management seasonal discharge policy as a contributing factor to the discharge pattern.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly self-flagged as the paper's own proposed explanation ("We propose that"), a moderate-confidence, own-reasoning-based account rather than a literature-validated one — an appropriately calibrated hedge given this is a genuinely novel mechanistic claim the paper is making for the first time about this specific hydrosystem.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A (internal puzzle-resolution, not literature comparison).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes the water-level-vs-discharge distinction used as the analytical basis for the rest of the Discussion's event-scale analysis.
8. **Limitations:** None explicit here.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic, self-flagged as the paper's own novel proposal.

#### Paragraph 4 (lines 1412–1442)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive/mechanistic — explains the central finding (river surge without coastal storm surge) via storm geometry, then validates the finding against two independent prior studies.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Statistically significant river surge but no coastal surge (§4.3 result), and skew-surge decomposition (Fig. 7d).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct mechanistic explanation grounded in the paper's own wind-vector data: "from the wind vector maps of Fig. 2b–d it can be seen that offshore winds are created in the Vung Tau region due to the direction and angle of approach of TY Usagi. Hence, throughout the event strong onshore winds never occurred, which explains the lack of storm surge on the coast." This is followed by two concordance validations: "This result **is in line with** the study of Takagi et al. (2014), which modeled storm surge in Vietnam and found that the potential maximum of the storm surge for the southern sections is rather low (0.7 m)..."; and "The lack of storm surge during TY Usagi is not unprecedented particularly along the Vietnam coast. Trinh et al. (2020) show that even some of the strongest historical TYs that hit Vietnam can show no record of high storm surges in certain locations..."
4. **Literature use:** Takagi et al. (2014) and Trinh et al. (2020) both used as concordant validation — the paper's own single-event finding (no storm surge for this typhoon) is shown to be consistent with, not an anomaly relative to, a broader documented pattern for the region.
5. **Certainty level:** The wind-geometry mechanism is stated with high confidence, directly grounded in the paper's own measured wind-vector data (not borrowed from literature) — a directly-demonstrated mechanism, distinct from attributed/borrowed mechanisms elsewhere in the corpus.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Closes with an explicit generalization/future-research statement: "This indicates that more studies following the methodology of this paper could be valuable if applied to other urban hydrological systems in Vietnam or elsewhere in Southeast Asia" — a methodological (not site-specific-policy) implication, distinct from the practical/policy implications seen in most other papers in the corpus.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic, directly-demonstrated (not attributed) mechanism + concordance validation.

#### Paragraph 5 (lines 1443–1462)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — explains the 16-hour rainfall-discharge lag via multiple candidate contributing factors, explicitly ranked by evidentiary strength, including one genuinely speculative mechanism clearly flagged as such.
2. **Finding it starts from:** 16-hour lag between peak rainfall and peak residual discharge at the upstream station.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Sequential multi-factor explanation, each introduced with a distinct confidence marker: flat terrain and vegetation interception (supported directly by the paper's own land-cover data, Fig. 8c — moderate-high confidence); widespread upstream flooding (supported by the paper's own MODIS analysis, though qualified: "This analysis proved difficult to interpret within the watershed area due to the presence of clouds throughout the event. **However**, we found evidence of widespread flooding immediately east of the watershed..." — an explicit data-quality limitation named and partially worked around within the same paragraph); and finally aquifer recharge, explicitly flagged as the most speculative: "**Another possible driver**... **might be due to** the recharge of the groundwater table functioning as a buffer... **This possibly indicates** a groundwater recharge phenomenon followed by a slow spill..." — three consecutive hedges ("possible," "might be," "possibly indicates") mark this as the weakest-evidence explanation in the paragraph, explicitly ranked below the terrain/vegetation and flooding explanations.
4. **Literature use:** Tu et al. (2022), Khai and Koontanakulvong (2015) support the groundwater-recharge/hydraulic-gradient claim as a documented regional phenomenon, applied here as a candidate (not confirmed) contributing mechanism for this specific event.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly multi-tiered within a single paragraph — terrain/vegetation (grounded in own data, higher confidence), upstream flooding (own data but qualified by a stated cloud-cover data-quality limitation), aquifer recharge (explicitly the most speculative, marked with three successive hedges) — a clear example of calibrating hedge strength to evidentiary basis paragraph-internally, not just paragraph-to-paragraph.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None yet — mechanistic explanation deferred to inform the following paragraph's discharge-evacuation discussion.
8. **Limitations:** The cloud-cover/MODIS-interpretability limitation is named and partially worked around within the same paragraph rather than deferred.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive/mechanistic, multi-tiered hedging within one paragraph — a more granular, within-paragraph version of Chounna Yemele's B5 two-tiered-hedging pattern (there, tiered across a primary/secondary mechanism split; here, tiered across three candidate contributing factors).

#### Paragraph 6 (lines 1463–1483)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — explains a low discharge-evacuation percentage via a dominant water-budget process, validated against an independent water-budget model for the same region.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Only 9.3% of available rainwater evacuated as residual discharge 41 hours after the rain event ended.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct attribution: "This low percentage of evacuation is partly explained by the important role that evapotranspiration plays in the climate of the region, which can effectively reduce the peak discharge of the river." This is then validated against an independent model: "Rujner and Goedecke (2016) use the water budget model ABIMO to describe the long-term annual means of the total runoff, surface runoff, evaporation and infiltration around HCMC. The results show that surface runoff takes 7% and infiltration 6%, and the remaining 87% is evapotranspiration, which **is in line with our findings**."
4. **Literature use:** Rujner and Goedecke (2016) — a concordance validation using a *different method* (long-term annual water-budget modeling) applied to the *same region* to cross-check a *single-event* finding from this paper; a methodologically distinct but geographically matched form of concordance validation, notable because it cross-validates across different temporal scales (long-term annual average vs. single 41-hour event window) and finds them numerically compatible.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated with moderate-high confidence, appropriately calibrated given the independent cross-validation despite the differing temporal scope of the comparison.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Concordant.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces that this specific event's water balance is not anomalous relative to the region's known long-term hydrological behavior.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, comparative/concordance-validating across differing temporal scales.

#### Paragraph 7 (lines 1484–1503)
1. **Function:** Discordant/unexpected-result handling — tests and confirms a modeling assumption (upper watershed has no discharge influence) via a null result, then identifies a specific confound (reservoir operational policy) that could complicate the paper's own discharge estimate.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The assumption, stated in Methods, that the upper watershed (regulated by the Dau Tieng reservoir) does not influence Saigon River discharge.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct null-result confirmation: "On 26 November 2018... the upper part of the watershed received heavy precipitation... **However, we found no discharge response** of the Saigon River..., which justifies assuming that the upper part of the watershed is not influencing the river's discharge" — a clean example of treating a null result as informative confirmation of a modeling assumption rather than an ambiguous non-finding. The paragraph then immediately complicates this by introducing reservoir-operation data as a potential confound: "It is thus possible that part of the residual discharge is a direct cause of the dam's discharge policies, which would make the evacuation of rainfall by the river even smaller" — an explicit, unresolved caveat on the paper's own §5 Paragraph 6 evacuation-percentage estimate.
4. **Literature use:** Dinh and Nguyen (2019) supplies the specific reservoir discharge-capacity figures (dry season 30 m³/s, wet season ~100 m³/s with peaks to 400 m³/s) used to quantify the confound's plausible magnitude.
5. **Certainty level:** The null result (no discharge response) is stated as directly observed fact; the reservoir-confound caveat is explicitly hedged ("It is thus possible that...") as an unresolved complication to the paper's own quantitative estimate, not a demonstrated correction.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The null result is treated as confirming, not undermining, a modeling assumption — an explicit example of correctly distinguishing "true absence of effect" from "failure to detect," since the paper has both a clear rainfall input and a clear absence of discharge response to rule on.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None explicit — this paragraph functions as a self-critical audit of the paper's own discharge-estimation assumptions.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The reservoir-discharge-policy confound is a self-identified, unresolved limitation on the precision of the paper's own residual-discharge/evacuation-percentage estimate from Paragraph 6 — an example of a limitation appearing not as a generic caveat but as a specific quantitative uncertainty bound on an already-reported number.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, null-result confirmation + self-critical limitation identification.

#### Paragraph 8 (lines 1504–1513, closing)
1. **Function:** Closing paragraph — a methodological limitation on the discharge-estimation approach itself, ending the Discussion on an explicit call for cautious interpretation rather than a synthesis or forward-looking statement.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The discharge-estimation method's dependence on river-slope calculations from paired water-level stations.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct explanatory chain for why the discharge estimate may be systematically weak: "The relatively weak hydraulic response **could be explained by** the fact that both stations have a quasi-simultaneous increase in water level which leads to a less steep slope and thus weaker discharge... **Thus**, the slope should remain relatively constant, and river discharge **might be underestimated**." Additional sources of error are then listed ("over sensitivity to the water level measurement error," "difficulty in capturing asymmetrical tide dynamics"), closing with an explicit interpretive-caution instruction: "**Therefore, the interpretation of these discharge estimations must be made carefully**."
4. **Literature use:** None in this paragraph — self-contained methodological reasoning.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly hedged throughout ("could be explained by," "might be underestimated") — appropriately tentative since this concerns the reliability of the paper's own core derived quantity (discharge), not an external comparison.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None — this paragraph ends the Discussion on a caution rather than an implication, a notable deviation from every other paper in the corpus, all of which close their Discussion (or Discussion-equivalent) with either a practical implication, a synthesis, or a forward-research call rather than an uncertainty caveat.
8. **Limitations bounding:** This is the terminal paragraph of the Discussion and it is entirely a limitations statement — the clearest case in the 9-paper corpus of a Discussion ending directly on a limitation with no subsequent synthesis or reassertion-of-contribution paragraph before the Conclusions section begins.
9. **Role:** Limitations, terminal position — flagged explicitly in Step 4 as a genuine outlier relative to the closing-move pattern otherwise observed across the corpus.

### PAPER 9 — Luu et al. 2024, `§5 Discussion` (standalone)

#### Paragraph 1 (lines 857–866)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting — states the general stakes of multi-hazard assessment, then immediately states the paper's own central qualitative finding (floods and wildfires have very different spatial footprints and very different impacts on the built environment) before any literature engagement.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The multi-hazard susceptibility matrix and exposure results (§4.4).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** General framing citing Menoni et al. (2012) for why multi-hazard assessment matters, followed by direct restatement of the paper's own finding: "floods and wildfires are examples of two hazards with different spatial patterns but quite similar spatial extent and frequency: assessment of the combined exposure to both hazards highlights that they have a very different impact on built-up infrastructure." The paragraph closes with an explicit scope-extension suggestion: "Additional hazards, such as landslides or droughts, should be added to the scheme, with a multi-dimension hazard matrix..." (citing Yousefi et al. 2020) — a forward-scope recommendation appearing very early in the Discussion rather than at the end.
4. **Literature use:** Menoni et al. (2012) for general framing; Yousefi et al. (2020) to support the multi-dimensional-matrix extension idea.
5. **Certainty level:** The paper's own finding is stated as directly demonstrated; the scope-extension suggestion is prescriptive/forward-looking, not a certainty claim.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The forward-scope-extension implication appears unusually early (first paragraph) relative to the corpus norm of implications following, not opening, the interpretive sequence.
8. **Limitations:** None explicit yet, though the "additional hazards should be added" statement implicitly flags the two-hazard scope as a limitation without naming it as such.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting, with an early, implicit scope-limitation signal folded into the framing rather than stated as a formal limitation.

#### Paragraph 2 (lines 881–887)
1. **Function:** Concordance/context paragraph — situates the paper's ML-modeling choice within the broader methodological literature before returning to the paper's own results.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own choice to use Random Forest and CART for susceptibility modeling.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** General literature-context statement ("ML models have been extensively used in diverse hazard evaluations, such as flood, landslide, and wildfire susceptibility," citing Bui et al. 2023a; Ha et al. 2022; Pourtaghi et al. 2016) followed by a methodological rationale for why comparing models under identical conditions is valuable ("ensures a fair and unbiased determination of the most appropriate model") — this paragraph does not yet engage the paper's own specific AUC results, functioning instead as a methodological-legitimacy paragraph.
4. **Literature use:** Bui et al. (2023a), Ha et al. (2022), Pourtaghi et al. (2016), Tavakkoli Piralilou et al. (2022) — all supporting general methodological precedent for the modeling approach, not yet validating specific numeric results.
5. **Certainty level:** General/established-practice statements, not hedged, appropriate for methodological-precedent claims.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None yet.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Orienting/methodological-legitimacy, positioned before the concordance-check on the paper's own specific results (which follows in Paragraph 3) — a methodological-context-first move.

#### Paragraph 3 (lines 888–898)
1. **Function:** Concordance-first validation of the paper's own headline model-performance results.
2. **Finding it starts from:** RF outperforming CART for both hazards (flood AUC 0.882/0.884 vs. wildfire AUC comparison; RF AUC 0.884 vs. CART AUC 0.846 for wildfire).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct concordance statement: "both models demonstrated good performance for flood and wildfire susceptibility maps, **aligning with earlier research findings** (Hasanzadeh Nafari et al., 2016; Nachappa et al., 2020)." The paragraph then attributes model accuracy to specific design choices made in this study (multicollinearity checking, variable-importance-based feature selection, careful non-hazard-point selection), framing the paper's own good performance as a consequence of methodological care rather than of the RF/CART algorithms alone.
4. **Literature use:** Hasanzadeh Nafari et al. (2016), Nachappa et al. (2020) — concordant validation of the general good-performance finding; Pourtaghi et al. (2016) re-cited to support the claim that accuracy depends on influencing-factor selection.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as directly demonstrated (own AUC results) plus literature-concordant validation — high confidence, appropriately so for a straightforward model-performance metric comparison.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes methodological credibility before the paper moves to interpreting the multi-hazard matrix's substantive (not just technical) findings.
8. **Limitations:** None in this paragraph.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, concordance-validating — the paper's A1-type move, appearing after (not before) the methodological-context paragraph.

#### Paragraph 4 (lines 899–918)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — interprets the multi-hazard matrix's central spatial finding (inverse flood/fire susceptibility relationship) and explicitly claims methodological novelty for the matrix-based (vs. additive) integration approach, then names a specific unaddressed factor (differential building vulnerability) that bounds the claim's completeness.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The inverse spatial pattern between flood and wildfire susceptibility (§4.4) and the disproportionate building-exposure-to-area ratios (Table 4).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct explanatory restatement of the matrix's advantage over a simpler alternative: "The multi-hazard map is built upon a susceptibility class matrix for flood and wildfire events **instead of** a simple summation of both susceptibility maps. Indeed, the matrix enables the identification of regions with different combinations of hazard susceptibility..." — an explicit method-choice justification (matrix vs. additive index) argued via what the matrix reveals that summation would not. The paragraph then makes an explicit novelty claim: "Creating a multi-hazard exposure map that effectively delineates regions susceptible to floods and wildfires via the implementation of a matrix-based approach and combining the map with built-environment data... **has not previously been attempted by other researchers**." This confident novelty claim is immediately qualified: "however, the contrasted vulnerability of buildings relative to different hazards, taking into account the specific attributes of the building, is also important in controlling the potential damage (Šakić Trogrlić et al., 2024). Such hazard-specific vulnerability functions for different building types **still need to be constrained** for the study area before a fully quantitative risk assessment can be completed."
4. **Literature use:** Šakić Trogrlić et al. (2024) is deployed to name a specific, structurally distinct limitation (building-type-specific vulnerability functions) that the paper's own exposure-only (not vulnerability-weighted) approach does not address — a scope-bounding literature use rather than a concordance or discordance use.
5. **Certainty level:** The novelty claim is stated with unhedged confidence ("has not previously been attempted"); the immediately following limitation is also stated directly, not softened — the pairing of a strong novelty claim with an immediate, specific scope caveat in the same paragraph is a distinctive rhetorical structure in this paper.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — the matrix-vs-summation argument functions as the paper's central methodological-contribution claim, immediately bounded by what it still cannot do (quantitative risk assessment without vulnerability functions).
8. **Limitations bounding:** The building-vulnerability-function gap is a locally-placed limitation, appearing directly adjacent to the specific claim (methodological novelty of the exposure map) it qualifies, in the local-limitation-placement style seen throughout the corpus (A4-type).
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, novelty-claim + immediately adjacent scope-limitation — a compact instance of the claim-then-bound pattern.

#### Paragraph 5 (lines 919–935)
1. **Function:** Attempted external validation against independent damage statistics, with an explicit, honest statement of why full validation is not possible.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own finding that building exposure to floods exceeds exposure to wildfires (Table 4).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** General statement on why validation matters (citing Skilodimou et al. 2019; Khan et al. 2020), followed by two independent provincial damage statistics presented as directionally consistent: "The 2020 flood and storm events caused 46 deaths; more than 117,000 properties to be flooded and damaged... over the past 5 years in Quang Nam, there have been 136 forest fires that caused damage to more than 618 ha... These available statistics **confirmed** the larger exposure of buildings to floods than to wildfires..." — but this "confirmed" is immediately and explicitly qualified: "**However the lack of damage statistics per hazard type at a fine spatial resolution prevents the comparison of** our multi-hazard exposure map with actual recorded damage." The paper uses coarse, non-gridded statistics for directional (flood > fire) confirmation only, explicitly declining to claim spatial validation the data cannot support.
