4. **Literature use:** VDMA (2020) and the Quang Nam Forest Protection Department's public statistics are used as independent (non-peer-reviewed, administrative) validation sources — a data-source type distinct from peer-reviewed literature citations used elsewhere, appropriate given the paper is seeking real-world damage-record validation rather than methodological precedent.
5. **Certainty level:** The directional claim (floods cause more building exposure than fires) is treated as confirmed at the coarse/aggregate level; the paper explicitly and precisely states what this validation cannot do (spatially resolved cross-validation against the exposure map) — a careful, non-overclaiming distinction between two different strengths of validation claim within the same paragraph.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Concordant at the aggregate/directional level; the paper does not claim concordance at a resolution finer than the validation data supports.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces the practical relevance of the flood-exposure-dominance finding for risk-management prioritization.
8. **Limitations bounding:** A precise, data-resolution-specific limitation (fine-spatial-resolution damage statistics unavailable) stated immediately adjacent to the claim it bounds — another local-limitation-placement instance (A4-type).
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, external validation attempt + explicit validation-limits statement — a careful, two-tier confirmation matching Chounna Yemele's §5.3 Para 3 GRACE-data-bounding move (state what the evidence *can* and *cannot* show) but here applied to damage statistics rather than satellite gravimetry.

#### Paragraph 6 (lines 936–957, closing)
1. **Function:** Closing paragraph — names two further, more conceptual limitations (lack of temporal/process-based hazard-interaction modeling; lack of stakeholder engagement) and closes the Discussion entirely on these unresolved gaps rather than on a synthesis or reassertion-of-contribution statement.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own static, non-temporal, non-interactive hazard-matrix design.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct scope statement: "The consideration of temporal relationships between hazards (i.e., fire during the dry season inducing flood in the next rain season) or non-local dynamic interactions (i.e., wildfire in upper catchment increasing flood occurrence downstream) **would require** more process-oriented hazard modeling at a more local scale" (citing Jenkins et al. 2023) — a forward-research-agenda statement rather than a claim about what the current paper demonstrated. A second, distinct limitation follows: "Another significant limitation of this research is the absence of the consideration of stakeholder engagement and feedback... Interaction with stakeholders in charge of risk management would help to identify further the challenges... validate the modeling approach..."
4. **Literature use:** Jenkins et al. (2023) supports the call for process-oriented, locally-scaled hazard-interaction modeling as the appropriate future-research direction.
5. **Certainty level:** Both limitations are stated plainly as acknowledged scope gaps, not hedged as uncertain about their existence.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The stakeholder-engagement limitation carries an implicit practical implication (future work should incorporate stakeholder validation) but this is framed as a research-process recommendation, not a direct action-now policy implication of the kind seen in most other papers' closing paragraphs.
8. **Limitations bounding:** Two distinct limitations named in sequence (temporal/process-interaction modeling scope; stakeholder-engagement absence), both left as open future-work items without a subsequent synthesis paragraph reconnecting to the paper's overall contribution.
9. **Role:** Limitations, terminal position — structurally similar to Rodrigues do Amaral et al.'s Discussion, which also ends directly on a limitations statement rather than a closing/synthesis move, making this the second of two papers in the 9-paper corpus whose Discussion ends on an unresolved limitation rather than a synthesis, reassertion-of-contribution, or forward-looking-but-affirmative statement (flagged explicitly in Step 4).

# Scratch: Paper 10 (and running) — Step 1 + Step 2 drafts

## PAPER 10: Strozzi et al. 2026 — L-band SAR interferometry for alpine rock slope instabilities

**Citation:** Strozzi, T., Jones, N., Agliardi, F., De Pedrini, A., Frey, O., Bernhard, P., Caduff, R., Ambrosi, C., and Manconi, A. (2026). *Monitoring the displacement of large alpine rock slope instabilities with L-band SAR interferometric techniques.* Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci., 26, 2579– (2026). URL: https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/26/2579/2026/

**Status: SUCCESS.** Full research article confirmed. Standalone Discussion (`6 Discussion`, subsections 6.1–6.3), separate from `5 Results`, followed by `7 Conclusion and Outlook`.

### STEP 1 — Foundations
- **Research question:** Can L-band SAR interferometry (ALOS-2 PALSAR-2, SAOCOM-1, and a terrestrial car-borne L-band system, GLSAR) overcome known shortcomings of C-band (Sentinel-1) interferometry — vegetation-induced decorrelation and saturation at high displacement rates — for detecting and monitoring large alpine rock-slope instabilities?
- **Objective:** Cross-compare L-band and C-band multi-temporal InSAR products (PSI, DInSAR, SBAS, stacking) against independent terrestrial validation data (GNSS, ground-based radar interferometry GB-DInSAR, Motorized Total Station, car-borne GLSAR) at three structurally and kinematically different alpine sites, to characterize where L-band adds value and where fundamental technique limits (not just wavelength) remain.
- **Study design:** Empirical multi-site, multi-sensor comparison. Three case-study slopes: Monte Mater (Italy, deep-seated slope deformation, <3 cm/yr to 10 mm/month), Val Canaria (Switzerland, rockslides/rockfalls in karst terrain, ~80 million m³, rates 10 cm/yr to 50–80 cm/day), Brienz/Brinzauls (Switzerland, large complex landslide, 1.2 Mm³ failure June 2023, velocities several m/yr). Satellite L-band (ALOS-2 PALSAR-2 2015–2023; SAOCOM-1 2021–2024) processed via PSI, standard DInSAR, DInSAR stacking, SBAS; compared against C-band Sentinel-1 (via EGMS/Geoportale Ticino/independent studies); validated against terrestrial GNSS, GB-DInSAR, MTS, and car-borne GLSAR at each site.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — three alpine instability complexes; validation points are terrestrial monitoring benchmarks (10 GNSS at Monte Mater, 3 GNSS at Brienz [1 usable], 2 MTS benchmarks at Val Canaria).
- **Key findings the Discussion must interpret:**
  - L-band PS density exceeds C-band by up to 20x at Monte Mater (10-15x in forest), ~5x at Val Canaria, notably higher at Brienz in forested sectors; C-band yields essentially no usable points in Val Canaria's vegetated slopes.
  - Monitoring accuracy is velocity-dependent, not just wavelength-dependent: <5 cm/yr — L-band PSI matches GNSS/GB-DInSAR closely; ~10 cm/yr — SAOCOM-1 SBAS follows trend but misses intra-annual variability; ~20 cm/yr — C-band recovers only ~25% of true displacement, L-band ~50%; at Brienz's fastest sectors (several m/yr) PSI (both bands) fails entirely — a technique ceiling, not a band-specific one.
  - At Val Canaria pt. 26, SAOCOM-1 SBAS matches MTS well 2020-2023 but underestimates a 2023-2024 acceleration, attributed to phase-unwrapping error.
  - Terrestrial GLSAR is shown to independently validate and extend satellite results, especially at Brienz's fastest, satellite-undetectable sectors.

### STEP 2 — Discussion paragraph-by-paragraph

#### §6.1 "L-band versus C-band" — Paragraph 1
1. **Function:** Orienting/methodological-setup — establishes the C-band comparison-data sources and the land-cover basis (forest %) for the site-by-site comparison to follow.
2. **Finding it starts from:** PS density histograms (Fig. 12) computed across all datasets, forested vs non-forested, per site.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Descriptive-factual chaining, no hedge: states data provenance (EGMS, Geoportale Ticino, Crippa et al. 2021, Strozzi et al. 2022), then states the headline comparative fact directly: "L-band acquisitions consistently provide much higher PS densities, in some cases up to twenty times greater than C-band, depending on the satellite platform, orbit direction, and number of images available."
4. **Literature use:** Crippa et al. (2021), Strozzi et al. (2022) cited as sources of the independent C-band comparison datasets — a data-provenance use, not a concordance/discordance comparison.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established, directly measured fact (own PS-density computation) — high confidence, appropriate since this is a direct data comparison, not an inferred mechanism.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up the per-site elaboration that follows.
8. **Limitations:** None yet.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting for §6.1.

#### §6.1 — Paragraph 2 (Monte Mater)
1. **Function:** Site-specific elaboration of the headline PS-density finding, explaining what the higher density enables (spatial resolvability of internal kinematic compartments).
2. **Finding it starts from:** Monte Mater PS-density ratios (up to 20x unforested, 10-15x forested).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct causal/enabling statement, unhedged: "This increase in point density at L-band allows the moving area of the DSGSD and its internal kinematic compartments to be resolved with a spatial continuity that is impossible to achieve with C-band."
4. **Literature use:** None new.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as fact, directly grounded in the paper's own PS-density data.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — L-band enables a qualitatively better map, not just more points.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, site-specific instance of the general finding.

#### §6.1 — Paragraph 3 (Val Canaria)
1. **Function:** Site-specific elaboration — a stronger, near-total-failure case for C-band, and a within-L-band comparison (SAOCOM-1 vs ALOS-2) attributing an advantage to temporal sampling rate rather than wavelength alone.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Val Canaria C-band near-total data void on vegetated slopes; L-band ~5x average density advantage.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct factual escalation ("limitations of C-band are even more apparent... no solution at all on vegetated slopes, leaving the mapped landslides essentially undetected"), then a within-technique mechanistic attribution: "the higher temporal sampling rate of SAOCOM-1 compared to ALOS-2 translates directly into a denser point distribution and better definition of slope sectors with more rapid motion" — isolates temporal sampling rate as a separate causal factor from wavelength, a finer-grained mechanistic distinction than the paper 1 (Monte Mater) paragraph made.
4. **Literature use:** None new.
5. **Certainty level:** Unhedged, direct causal statement ("translates directly into") — grounded in the paper's own comparative dataset (both are the paper's own processed products, so directly demonstrated).
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — foreshadows the multi-frequency/multi-sampling-rate integration argument of §6.3.
8. **Limitations:** None yet.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, mechanistic — separates two conflated causal factors (wavelength vs. revisit time).

#### §6.1 — Paragraph 4 (Brienz/Brinzauls)
1. **Function:** Discordant/complicating finding handling — an exception to the "L-band always denser" pattern is named directly, explained mechanistically, and then generalized into a technique-level (not wavelength-level) limitation that both bands share.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Brienz PS density: L-band clearly better than C-band overall, BUT non-forested (high-velocity) areas show LOWER L-band point density than expected.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct explanation for the anomaly: "The reason of lower point densities in non-forested areas is because these areas are also the ones in the central body of the landslide experiencing the highest velocities... Where displacements reached very high values, coherence is lost in both frequency bands and no PSI points are retrieved." This is immediately generalized: "This reflects a fundamental limit of the PSI technique rather than a wavelength effect: once velocities exceed a certain threshold, neither C- nor L-band can provide stable measurements." This generalizing move — converting a site-specific anomaly into a stated technique-level boundary condition — is a distinctive rhetorical structure: the paper does not let a potentially confusing result sit unexplained, and uses it to sharpen the paper's own central claim (this is about PSI's decorrelation limit, not just wavelength).
4. **Literature use:** None in this specific passage (mechanism argued from the paper's own data and known PSI theory).
5. **Certainty level:** Stated with high confidence/unhedged ("This reflects a fundamental limit," "neither C- nor L-band can provide") — appropriate because it is a well-established property of PSI coherence loss at high deformation gradients, not a novel or speculative claim.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** This is the paper's clearest discordant-finding-handling paragraph in §6.1 — an exception to the general L-band-superiority pattern is named plainly and explained rather than smoothed over or omitted.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Direct methodological implication follows immediately: "Due to the limitations of PSI for the detection of high displacement rates, alternative techniques such as standard DInSAR or stacking can be employed" — followed by reasserting L-band's advantage in that alternative-technique context too ("L-band can better retrieve spatially distributed information and capture various processes acting at different velocities").
8. **Limitations bounding:** The PSI-technique velocity ceiling is stated as a general, acknowledged limitation of the method itself (not just this paper's application of it), immediately paired with a compensating alternative (DInSAR/stacking) — a limitation-then-workaround move.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, discordance-handling + limitation-with-workaround, closing §6.1.

#### §6.2 "Monitoring Performance" — Paragraph 1 (Monte Mater)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — validates L-band PSI time series against independent terrestrial ground-truth (GNSS, GB-DInSAR), explicitly stratified by velocity regime, with an honest, graded account of where the match degrades.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Comparison of PSI-derived displacement time series against GNSS/GB-DInSAR at three velocity regimes (<5 cm/yr, ~10 cm/yr, ~20 cm/yr).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** A velocity-stratified concordance/discordance ladder, not a single yes/no validation: "For sectors moving at less than 5 cm/yr, L-band PSI provides displacement time series in close agreement to GNSS and GB-DInSAR... C-band time series are more affected by sparse coverage and fail to reproduce these non-linear trends" (concordant for L-band at low velocity, discordant for C-band even at low velocity); "At intermediate velocities of about 10 cm/yr, SAOCOM-1 PSI continues to follow the slightly non-linear behaviour... although intra-annual variations are not fully resolved" (partial concordance, explicitly qualified); "At higher velocities (~20 cm/yr), all satellite PSI products underestimate motion: C-band captures only about 25% of the true displacement, while L-band recovers around 50%" (quantified, graded failure for both bands, L-band still better but explicitly imperfect). This is a systematic three-tier validation report, each tier given its own explicit, numerically bounded verdict rather than one blended summary statement.
4. **Literature use:** Notti et al. (2014) supports the LOS-projection methodological choice (projecting onto steepest slope to reconcile satellite/terrestrial LOS differences); Manconi (2021) is cited for the theoretical velocity-detection-threshold tables (deferred to Appendix D) — both supporting-methodology citations, not comparative validation citations.
5. **Certainty level:** Each velocity tier's claim is stated with a precision matched to what the paired terrestrial data can show — a directly demonstrated, quantitatively bounded finding (percent of true displacement recovered), not hedged as speculative, because GNSS/GB-DInSAR provide a genuine independent ground truth at this site.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** A graded concordance-to-discordance spectrum across velocity tiers, explicitly quantified at the top end (25% vs. 50% recovery) rather than declared as a simple pass/fail — the paper does not claim L-band "solves" the high-velocity underestimation problem, only that it mitigates it by roughly a factor of two.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes the velocity-dependent reliability envelope that becomes the paper's central practical message (stated explicitly in §6.3 and the Conclusion).
8. **Limitations bounding:** The high-velocity underestimation (even for L-band, ~50% recovery) is stated as a quantified, unresolved measurement gap, not glossed over.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, systematic graded validation — this paragraph structurally resembles a stacked series of A1 (concordance-check) and A2 (discordant-handling) moves collapsed into one paragraph, organized by velocity regime rather than by comparator study.

#### §6.2 — Paragraph 2 (Brienz/Brinzauls)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — validates L-band at moderate velocity, then explicitly reports where satellite PSI fails entirely and terrestrial data becomes the sole informative source, functioning as a discordant/technique-limit paragraph resolved by a specific compensating data source rather than left as a gap.
2. **Finding it starts from:** GNSS comparison at Brienz (1 usable of 3 stations), pre- and post-2019 velocity regimes.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Concordant statement first: "In 2018–2019, for LOS rates of motion of about 5 cm/yr, the L-band time series matches GNSS displacements... confirming the reliability of the method under moderate velocities." Then an explicit velocity-driven breakdown: "After 2019, however, slope acceleration reached several metres per year, well beyond the measurable range of PSI. L-band results then strongly underestimate the real displacements and the fastest slope sectors yielded no PSI points at all." The paragraph then names the compensating data source directly: "the dense terrestrial network of GNSS, GB-DInSAR, and car-borne GLSAR provided the critical information."
4. **Literature use:** None new in this paragraph.
5. **Certainty level:** The moderate-velocity concordance is stated as directly confirmed; the post-2019 breakdown is stated as established fact (a technique ceiling being exceeded, not a hypothesis).
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Explicit two-phase result: concordant pre-2019, then a clean technique-failure (not ambiguous partial degradation, but total loss of PSI points) post-2019 — the sharpest discordant/breakdown result in the paper, immediately paired with the specific alternative data source that filled the resulting gap (a limitation-then-workaround move).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces the paper's central practical argument that satellite InSAR alone (at any wavelength) cannot monitor the fastest-moving, highest-risk slope sectors, motivating the multi-sensor integration argument of §6.3.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The PSI technique-ceiling limitation is named plainly and precisely (velocity exceeding "several metres per year"), immediately compensated by naming the terrestrial network that substitutes for it.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, discordance-then-workaround.

#### §6.2 — Paragraph 3 (Val Canaria / Rütan dei Sass)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — a finer-grained, two-point quantitative validation (fast-moving vs. slow-moving points) against an independent geodetic method (MTS), including one explicitly attributed data-processing error and a direct numeric comparison at the slow-moving point.
2. **Finding it starts from:** MTS surveys at two benchmarks (pt. 26 fast-moving, pt. 25 slow-moving), spanning 2013–2024.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** For pt. 26: concordance stated first ("aligns very well with the MTS measurements... between 2020 and 2023"), then a discordant finding named and explained: "the strong acceleration between 2023 and 2024, although detected with the SAOCOM-1 SBAS analysis, is underestimated. Phase unwrapping errors in the interferograms spanning this time period are **likely** the reason of the discrepancy" — an explicit, hedged ("likely") attribution to a specific, named processing artifact rather than a vague "measurement error." For pt. 25: a direct numeric comparison table embedded in prose (MTS: 15 mm/yr 2018-2024, 10 mm/yr 2020-2024; satellite: 12 mm/yr 2014-2024, 9 mm/yr 2020-2024) framed as "compare well," followed immediately by a named exception ("more noise and general deviations, e.g., in the period from October 2019 to October 2020, when a significant acceleration detected by the MTS was not recorded by ALOS-2 PALSAR-2").
4. **Literature use:** Ng et al. (2012) supports the ~9 mm/yr figure as the expected L-band detection-accuracy threshold — used to contextualize why the pt. 25 comparison shows some noise (the measured rate is near the sensor's detection floor); Nishiguchi et al. (2017) supports the ~15 mm single-measurement accuracy figure — both citations used to calibrate expectations for how good the agreement "should" be, rather than to validate/contrast the finding against another study's site.
5. **Certainty level:** The processing-error attribution is explicitly hedged ("likely the reason"), appropriately since this is the paper's own inference about the cause of a discrepancy in its own processing, not an independently confirmed fact. The near-detection-threshold framing (Ng et al. 2012) is used to explain the noisier pt. 25 comparison with an established sensor-physics fact, not a hedge.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** A clean example of a paper reporting a partial/imperfect validation honestly — good agreement in most of the record, one specific named period/mechanism of disagreement (phase unwrapping, October 2019–2020 gap), rather than an unqualified "matches well" claim.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces the message that L-band accuracy is good near but not below its physical detection floor, and that specific processing artifacts (phase unwrapping under strong acceleration) are an identifiable, not mysterious, source of residual error.
8. **Limitations bounding:** The phase-unwrapping-error explanation functions as a locally-placed, specific limitation on the pt. 26 result; the Ng et al. (2012) detection-threshold citation functions similarly for pt. 25, precisely bounding what precision is achievable given the underlying sensor physics.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, fine-grained quantitative validation with explicit, attributed sources of residual disagreement — closes §6.2.

#### §6.3 "Towards Multi-Frequency Integration" — Paragraph 1 (opening)
1. **Function:** Transitional/orienting for the final Discussion subsection — states that the results demonstrate both "advantages and limitations," explicitly signaling that the subsection to follow will synthesize rather than introduce new findings.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The cumulative site-by-site findings of §6.1–6.2.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct synthetic statement, no hedge: "clearly illustrate the advantages and limitations of satellite and terrestrial L-band datasets... Each of the three study sites highlights important aspects of our findings."
4. **Literature use:** None.
5. **Certainty level:** N/A — framing statement.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up per-site synthesis.
8. **Limitations:** None yet (though "limitations" is named explicitly as a co-equal theme with "advantages" from the first sentence — an early signal this subsection will not oversell the method).
9. **Role:** Opening/transitional for the closing synthesis subsection.

#### §6.3 — Paragraph 2 (Monte Mater synthesis)
1. **Function:** Site-level synthesis — restates Monte Mater's core message (L-band as complementary, not replacement, data) as a general takeaway.
2. **Finding it starts from:** §6.1/6.2's Monte Mater-specific findings.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Balanced framing: "some information can be extracted from C-band PSI results... but the point coverage remains too scarce to clearly outline the full extent... L-band results... strongly complement the C-band data." The word "complement," not "replace," is the load-bearing framing choice here.
4. **Literature use:** None new.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established, directly demonstrated conclusion from the paper's own comparison.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — L-band's practical role is as an addition to existing C-band monitoring infrastructure (e.g., EGMS), not a wholesale replacement, an implication with direct relevance to future monitoring-program design.
8. **Limitations:** None new.
9. **Role:** Closing synthesis, site-specific.

#### §6.3 — Paragraph 3 (Val Canaria synthesis)
1. **Function:** Site-level synthesis — generalizes the temporal-sampling-rate finding (from §6.1 Para 3) into an explicit forward-looking design recommendation and names the specific remaining gap (fast-motion detection) plus its proposed technical remedy.
2. **Finding it starts from:** §6.1/6.2 Val Canaria findings (C-band failure, SAOCOM-1 vs ALOS-2 sampling-rate advantage).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Restates the "no information... at Val Canaria" C-band failure directly, then generalizes: "These findings suggest that with a sufficiently high temporal sampling, satellite-based L-band data can provide a good understanding of inter-annual changes... and may act as valuable information in monitoring programmes" — an explicit hedge ("may act as," "suggest") appropriately calibrated since this is a generalization from one site to future monitoring-program design. A remaining gap is named directly: "gaps remain which are related to the limited velocity range generally detectable with PSI," with an explicit remedy: "These can be filled with the use of differential interferograms and other advanced DInSAR techniques such as SBAS."
4. **Literature use:** None new in this paragraph.
5. **Certainty level:** Moderately hedged for the forward-looking generalization ("suggest," "may act as"); the gap-and-remedy statement is stated more directly since it is grounded in the paper's own demonstrated use of SBAS at this exact site.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Direct methodological/practical implication for monitoring-program design (adequate temporal sampling rate is necessary, not just L-band vs. C-band choice).
8. **Limitations bounding:** The PSI velocity-range gap is restated here as a persisting limitation even after the sampling-rate improvement, immediately paired with a named compensating technique (SBAS/differential interferograms) — consistent with the limitation-then-workaround pattern seen earlier.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive synthesis + limitation-with-workaround.

#### §6.3 — Paragraph 4 (GLSAR cross-validation)
1. **Function:** Cross-cutting synthesis (not site-specific) — argues for the terrestrial GLSAR instrument's general reliability by showing consistency with satellite results even under adverse local conditions.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Comparison of GLSAR-derived displacement maps against satellite-derived maps at Monte Mater and Val Canaria.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct concordance statement: "The displacement maps obtained with the car-borne instrument are very similar to the satellite-based results, even in Val Canaria despite difficult local conditions, such as a rough road..." — the "even... despite" construction functions as a robustness argument (concordance holding under a specifically adverse condition strengthens the claim more than concordance under ideal conditions would).
4. **Literature use:** None new.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as directly demonstrated (own comparative data), high confidence.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant, used to argue reciprocal validation value between the two measurement types ("show the reciprocal validation potential of satellite- and ground-based data studies").
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — supports future use of GLSAR as an independent check on satellite products in future monitoring designs.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, cross-site synthesis, concordance-as-robustness-argument.

#### §6.3 — Paragraph 5 (Brienz synthesis, closing)
1. **Function:** Closing synthesis for the whole Discussion — restates Brienz as the paper's clearest case for multi-sensor necessity, explicitly reasserts the technique-ceiling limitation established in §6.1/6.2, and closes with a forward-looking methodological/technological pointer.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The cumulative Brienz findings (PSI point-density advantage of L-band; total PSI failure at the fastest sectors; terrestrial GLSAR filling the gap).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Restates the PS-density advantage, then restates the technique ceiling directly and unhedged: "the limitations of PSI regardless of the sensor wavelength... are also apparent, as the Brienz landslide itself cannot be detected with this technique even at L-band" — explicitly generalizing the §6.1 Paragraph 4 finding (that the ceiling is technique-level, not wavelength-level) as the paper's clearest illustrative case. Followed by an explicit call for a different technique: "A multi-technique approach through use of differential interferograms and other advanced DInSAR techniques is thus necessary... In particular, the use of offset-tracking approaches could add valuable insight (Manconi et al., 2024b)." The paragraph then names the terrestrial GLSAR's specific contribution at the exact sectors satellite data cannot resolve, before a final forward-looking sentence about future satellite capability: "Satellite data available with sub-daily revisit times and at sub-metric spatial resolutions can also be a viable alternative to manage slope instability operational scenarios (Manconi et al., 2024b)."
4. **Literature use:** Manconi et al. (2024b) cited twice — once to support the offset-tracking-technique recommendation, once to support a forward-looking claim about future satellite capability — both prescriptive/forward-looking citation uses rather than concordance/discordance validation.
5. **Certainty level:** The technique-ceiling restatement is unhedged (established from the paper's own repeated demonstration across two subsections); the forward-looking capability claim is appropriately hedged as a future possibility ("can also be a viable alternative"), not a demonstrated result of this study.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A — synthesis of already-established findings.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** The paragraph's implication is explicitly methodological/technological (recommends specific alternative techniques and future sensor capabilities) rather than a site-management policy recommendation — consistent with this paper's technical/instrumental research question (which technique works, not what to do about the hazard).
8. **Limitations bounding:** The PSI technique-ceiling is the paper's single most heavily repeated limitation (stated in §6.1 Para 4, §6.2 Para 2, and here in §6.3 Para 5) — each repetition ties it to a different specific evidentiary instance (Brienz coherence loss, Brienz post-2019 GNSS mismatch, Brienz final synthesis) rather than being a generic disclaimer repeated verbatim.
9. **Role:** Closing synthesis for the entire Discussion — ends on a forward-looking technological/methodological note (not an unresolved limitation, and not a policy implication), consistent with a purely closing-move type (a) [reassertion of contribution/method] blended with a light touch of (b) [forward-looking capability pointer], per the existing file's Step 4 closing-move typology.

**Note on this paper's structural character relative to the 9-paper corpus:** This is a methods/instrument-validation paper, not a hazard-attribution or societal-impact paper. Its Discussion has almost no A3-type (attributed external mechanism) or A5-type (practical/policy implication) content in the sense the other 9 papers show — implications here are methodological/technological (which technique to combine, what future sensors to expect), not policy/practice recommendations for stakeholders managing risk. This is itself informative for the cross-paper Step 3 test of A5 (implications tracking whether the finding bears on a decision-relevant, actionable quantity for a non-technical stakeholder) — here the "stakeholder" is essentially the InSAR-monitoring community itself, and the actionable quantity is "which sensor/technique combination to deploy," a different register from extraction rates or design elevations elsewhere in the corpus. A1 (concordance-first validation) is very strongly present but restructured as within-paper, cross-instrument validation (satellite vs. terrestrial ground truth) rather than against external published literature — an important structural variant to flag in Step 3.

## PAPER 11: Bendoni et al. 2026 — Multiscale modeling of urban coastal flood events (SCORE Project)

**Citation:** Bendoni, M., Caparrini, F., Cucco, A., Taddei, S., Anton, I., Paranunzio, R., Mocali, R., Perna, M., Sacco, M., Vitale, G., Corongiu, M., Ortolani, A., Gharbia, S., and Brandini, C. (2026). *Multiscale modeling for coastal cities: addressing climate change impacts on flood events at urban-scale.* Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci., 26, 709– (2026). URL: https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/26/709/2026/

**Status: SUCCESS**, with one caveat on source-text fidelity: full verbatim text was recovered for most of the Discussion's Massa-specific paragraphs and for several individual sentences elsewhere (Vilanova, Oarsoaldea, limitations), confirmed by exact-quote WebFetch passes and cross-checked for citation integrity. For a portion of the model-limitations material and the transition/closing paragraphs, WebFetch's summarizer repeatedly declined full verbatim reproduction (citing copyright/length policy) despite the article being CC-BY 4.0, and returned topically organized direct quotes of individual sentences rather than complete sequential paragraphs. Where the analysis below relies on a sentence confirmed verbatim (in quotation marks, sourced from a directly-quoted WebFetch pass), it is treated as reliable evidence of hedge language. Where paragraph-level sequencing or the full connecting prose could not be recovered, this is marked explicitly `[KHÔNG XÁC ĐỊNH ĐƯỢC — full paragraph sequence not recovered verbatim]` rather than reconstructed. Standalone Discussion (`7 Discussion`), separate from `6 Results`, followed by `8 Conclusion and outlook`.

### STEP 1 — Foundations
- **Research question:** Can a multiscale numerical modeling chain — coupling regional-scale wave/water-level/hydrology models with high-resolution urban hydrodynamic simulation — quantify how climate change (RCP4.5, RCP8.5) will alter coastal and riverine flood extent/volume at the scale of individual coastal cities, bridging synoptic-scale (~100–1000 km) atmospheric forcing down to ~2 m urban-scale flood mapping?
- **Objective:** Build and demonstrate a generalizable multiscale/multi-model coupling chain (WaveWatch III → SHYFEM → LISFLOOD → HEC-RAS, forced by the ALADIN63 regional climate model under EURO-CORDEX) for three structurally different European coastal cities, producing return-period coastal and riverine flood-volume/extent estimates for Historical, RCP4.5, and RCP8.5 scenarios across two future time windows.
- **Study design:** Empirical multi-model, multi-site scenario-modeling study (not an experiment) — four coupled numerical models forced by a single RCM/GCM pair (ALADIN63/CNRM-CM5) due to data-availability and computational-cost constraints; extreme value analysis (GEV) on ~300 simulated years per model per city to derive 25- and 100-year return-period design events; hydrodynamic simulation (HEC-RAS) at urban resolution to map flooded area/volume under each scenario, benchmarked against the Historical period.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — three coastal cities under the SCORE Project (Climate-Change-Living-Labs, CCLLs): Massa (Italy), Vilanova i la Geltrù (Spain), Oarsoaldea (Spain); historical period 1973–2005, future windows 2011–2060 and 2051–2100.
- **Key findings the Discussion must interpret:**
  - Massa: minimal change in total water-level return-period magnitude (−3.5% to +3.2%) despite projected wave-height increases regionally, but large increases in flooded volume (7–60% coastal; 7–277% riverine) — a disconnect between "extreme value magnitude barely changes" and "flooded volume increases a lot," requiring explanation.
  - Vilanova: coastal flooding minimally affected by storm surge (topographically protected); riverine flood volumes increase 8–33% except one scenario showing a decrease (−20%, RCP4.5 2011–2060).
  - Oarsoaldea: both coastal (≤3% volume change) and riverine (decrease, −38% to −11%) flooding show the smallest projected changes of the three cities, attributed to port engineering design and a generalized decrease in peak river discharge.
  - Uncertainty (95% CI width) scales with the magnitude of projected change — largest for Massa's riverine projections, where the CI width is described as nearly 1.5x the estimated 100-year return-period value itself for RCP4.5/RCP8.5 2051–2100.

### STEP 2 — Discussion paragraph-by-paragraph

#### §7 Paragraph 1 (opening)
1. **Function:** Opening/orienting — restates the paper's own methodological ambition (bridging synoptic-to-urban scale) and immediately names the computational-cost trade-off this ambition required, before any specific finding is discussed.
2. **Finding it starts from:** No specific numeric finding yet — the paper's own modeling-chain design choice.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct, unhedged framing statement ("requires a huge effort due to the need to integrate processes across multiple scales"), followed by a factual description of the coupling chain, then a direct cost admission: "this level of detail comes with a huge computational effort: each of the three models ran simulations equivalent to nearly 300 years, repeated for all analyzed cities."
4. **Literature use:** None in this paragraph.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as plain fact (a description of the paper's own design and its computational cost), not hedged.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up Paragraph 2's justification of the single-RCM choice as a direct consequence of this cost constraint.
8. **Limitations/uncertainty bounding:** The computational-cost statement functions as an implicit limitation-framing device, similar in placement (Discussion's very first paragraph) to Rodrigues do Amaral (Paper 8, original 9-paper file) naming its short-record-length limitation in the opening paragraph.
9. **Role:** Opening/orienting, with an early limitation-as-framing move.

#### §7 Paragraph 2
1. **Function:** Direct methodological limitation, explicitly named and immediately paired with a partial compensating design choice — a limitation-then-workaround paragraph appearing in the Discussion's second paragraph.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own choice to use a single RCM/GCM pair rather than a multi-model ensemble.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct causal statement: "This consideration [computational cost] was the most significant factor influencing our choice of using a single RCM (and GCM) rather than a multi-model ensemble approach." Data-availability is named as a second, independent justification (only ALADIN63 provided all required variables across all four needed run types). The paragraph then explicitly names the resulting limitation and its partial mitigation: "We have given priority to have a continuous dataset at the cost of giving up an uncertainty estimate based on a multi-model ensemble. We tried to partially compensate for the lack of such an uncertainty estimation, by calculating confidence intervals through the bootstrap method... although this is a different source of uncertainty" — an explicit, self-aware admission that the compensating method (bootstrap CIs) addresses a *different* kind of uncertainty than the one it is substituting for, not a full fix.
4. **Literature use:** None in this paragraph (EURO-CORDEX/ALADIN63 are described as data sources, not comparator literature).
5. **Certainty level:** Directly and plainly stated as an acknowledged trade-off, not hedged as uncertain about whether the constraint exists; the final clause ("although this is a different source of uncertainty") is a notably precise, non-overclaiming qualification of the workaround's actual scope.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — signals to the reader that all downstream return-period estimates carry an unquantified inter-model uncertainty component in addition to the reported bootstrap CIs.
8. **Limitations bounding:** A clean example of the limitation-then-workaround pattern (C2 in the 9-paper file), with an unusually candid final admission that the workaround is imperfect even on its own terms.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, limitation-with-honest-partial-workaround, positioned very early (paragraph 2 of the Discussion).

#### §7 Paragraph 3
1. **Function:** Short methodological bridge paragraph — states the comparative basis (Evaluation run vs. Historical run) that the following city-specific paragraphs will rely on for validating the hazard maps.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The Evaluation-vs-Historical annual-maxima comparison (Fig. 5) and Tables 4–6.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Plain descriptive statement of what the comparison "enables us to assess" — no interpretation of a specific result yet.
4. **Literature use:** None.
5. **Certainty level:** N/A — methodological statement.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up the following paragraphs' reliability assessment.
8. **Limitations:** None new.
9. **Role:** Orienting/transitional, bridging the general limitations discussion (Paras 1–2) to the site-specific interpretation that follows.

#### §7 Paragraph 4 (Massa — coastal water level)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — resolves an apparent puzzle (near-zero change in extreme total-water-level magnitude despite a documented regional wave-height increase) via a multi-factor physical mechanism, then pivots to identify the actual dominant driver of the flood-volume increase.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Massa total-water-level return-period values showing minimal change (−3.5% to +3.2%) versus the Historical period.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** An explicit puzzle-then-resolution structure: states the counter-intuitive result plainly, notes the apparent contradiction ("Although an increase in wave height is projected for the Ligurian-Tyrrhenian Sea (De Leo et al., 2024), several factors **may contribute to** the observed invariance..."), then offers three converging physical mechanisms in sequence — shallow-bathymetry wave filtering, high modeling resolution capturing local effects "often missed by lower-resolution models," and quantified runup-sensitivity damping (a 1 m wave-height increase yields only 0.2–0.25 m runup increase, "calculated using wavelengths ranging between 70 and 95 m"). The paragraph then pivots explicitly: "Actually, the main driver behind the significant differences in flooded volume is the Relative Sea Level Rise (RSLR)... which allows storm surges to penetrate farther inland" — separating two different questions (why doesn't extreme water level change much? why does flooded volume change a lot?) and giving each its own distinct, quantitatively grounded answer.
4. **Literature use:** De Leo et al. (2024) is used to establish the background expectation (regional wave-height increase) that the paper's own local finding appears to contradict — a discordance-setup use, not a validating one; IPCC (2023) is then cited to validate the RSLR-as-dominant-driver conclusion: "This finding **is consistent with the conclusions of** the IPCC Sixth Assessment Report (IPCC, 2023), which states that 'regional sea level change will be the primary factor contributing to a substantial increase in the frequency of extreme still water levels...'" — a directly quoted concordance citation, one of very few instances across the 15-paper corpus where the paper quotes the comparator source's own words rather than paraphrasing it.
5. **Certainty level:** The three-factor runup-damping mechanism is stated with moderate-high confidence, hedged once ("several factors may contribute") but then argued through with specific quantitative support (own model's runup-sensitivity calculation); the RSLR-as-main-driver conclusion is stated with high confidence and directly validated against an authoritative external source (IPCC AR6).
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The paragraph opens by explicitly setting up an apparent discordance (regional wave increase vs. local water-level invariance) and resolves it analytically through the paper's own physical reasoning, rather than leaving it as an unexplained anomaly — structurally similar to Li et al.'s (Paper 5, original file) resolution of the non-monotonic hazard-probability pattern via a necessary-but-not-sufficient reframing.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes that RSLR uncertainty, not wave-climate uncertainty, is the dominant lever on Massa's coastal flood risk, which is echoed in Paragraph 5's uncertainty-propagation statement.
8. **Limitations:** None new in this specific paragraph (though the runup formula's own limitations are named later).
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, discordance-resolution + mechanistic, concordance-validated by an authoritative external source.

#### §7 Paragraph 5 (Massa — sea level rise uncertainty propagation)
1. **Function:** Direct extension of Paragraph 4's RSLR-as-dominant-driver finding into an explicit statement bounding how much of the paper's own downstream uncertainty is attributable to a single external input (sea-level-rise projections), including a tail-risk caveat.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The RSLR-as-dominant-driver conclusion of Paragraph 4.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct logical extension: "Therefore, all uncertainties in sea level rise projections can significantly affect the flood extension and volume." A tail-risk caveat follows: "the projected sea level rise by the end of the century could be significantly higher if the less likely, but still plausible, ice-sheet-related dynamics were to occur (IPCC, 2023, 2014)."
4. **Literature use:** IPCC (2023, 2014) cited to support the ice-sheet-dynamics tail-risk scenario as a recognized, if lower-probability, possibility — an appropriately hedged ("less likely, but still plausible") extension beyond the paper's own central scenario range (RCP4.5/RCP8.5 with standard SLR projections).
5. **Certainty level:** The main statement is stated with high confidence (a direct logical consequence of Paragraph 4); the ice-sheet tail-risk caveat is explicitly and doubly hedged ("less likely, but still plausible") — an appropriately calibrated acknowledgment that the paper's own scenario range likely does not capture the full uncertainty envelope.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — a scope caveat on how the paper's own numeric ranges should be read (as conditional on the SLR scenarios used, not an upper bound on possible outcomes).
8. **Limitations bounding:** This paragraph functions entirely as an uncertainty-bounding statement, locally placed immediately after the finding (RSLR dominance) it qualifies.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, limitation/uncertainty-bounding, tightly coupled to the preceding paragraph.

#### §7 Paragraph 6 (Massa — riverine floods)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — reports a large projected increase in riverine flood extremes, immediately paired with an equally large increase in reported uncertainty, then explicitly reasons about a possible systematic bias (underestimation) and its likely direction using a specific methodological assumption about the Evaluation run's reliability.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Substantial riverine-flood-volume increases in Massa's RCP scenarios, most pronounced for 2051–2100.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct concordant/quantified statement first, immediately qualified: "this is accompanied by an equal increase in uncertainty. Indeed, the width of the 95% CI is almost 1.5 times the 100 years RP for both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 2051–2100" — an unusually candid quantitative admission that the uncertainty band is comparable in size to the estimate itself. Despite this, the paper still asserts a directional conclusion ("Despite this, the overall increase in extreme Q_RP for all the analyzed scenarios and time windows confirms an increase in future peak river discharges"), then adds a further complication: "such extremes could be slightly underestimated as observable from the QQ-plot of Evaluation and Historical annual maxima." The reasoning for treating this as an underestimation (rather than an ambiguous mismatch) is stated explicitly: "we make the hypothesis that the Evaluation run, being a reanalysis, is close to reality given that it benefits from a data assimilation procedure" — an explicitly flagged hypothesis ("we make the hypothesis"), not a demonstrated fact.
4. **Literature use:** None new in this paragraph (self-contained statistical/own-data reasoning).
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly multi-tiered: the directional increase in Q_RP is stated with moderate-high confidence despite a very wide uncertainty band ("despite this... confirms an increase"); the underestimation-bias claim is explicitly and directly flagged as a hypothesis ("we make the hypothesis that"), the clearest self-labeled hypothesis-language instance in this paper's Discussion.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The paper does not suppress the fact that its own uncertainty band is comparable in magnitude to the point estimate — a transparent handling of a genuinely weak-confidence result, choosing to still assert a directional (not magnitude-precise) conclusion rather than either overclaiming precision or discarding the finding.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Deferred to the closing sentence of the paragraph, which links the riverine and coastal (RSLR) mechanisms together: "their impact on the ground is further augmented by the increase in relative sea level, whereby the higher downstream boundary condition hinders the flow toward the sea, resulting in a substantial increase in the flooded volume" — an explicit compounding/interaction mechanism between the two hazard drivers (riverine discharge and RSLR), reinforcing that Massa's flood risk is driven by a two-driver interaction, not either mechanism alone.
8. **Limitations:** The wide-CI admission and the explicitly hypothesized (not demonstrated) underestimation bias both function as locally placed, precisely stated limitations on how confidently the riverine numbers should be read.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, transparent uncertainty-handling + compounding-mechanism close.

#### §7 — Vilanova paragraph(s)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — explains Vilanova's near-total insulation from coastal flooding via a specific geomorphic mechanism (elevation gradient separating beach from urban area), then reports the riverine result with one explicitly named exception scenario.
2. **Finding it starts from:** Vilanova's minimal coastal-flood-volume change and mixed riverine-flood-volume results (mostly increases, one decrease).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct mechanistic attribution, unhedged, confirmed verbatim: "The extension of the flooded area for Vilanova appears not to be affected by storm surges principally due to the characteristics of the beach zone which is separated from the urban area by a steep positive gradient in the land elevation which makes the latter higher." For riverine flooding, the result is reported with its full range including the exception, confirmed verbatim: "The riverine floods associated with projections are generally characterized by an increase in flooded volume with respect to the Historical (from +11% to +33%), but for the RCP4.5 2011–2060 25 and 100 years RPs (−8% and −20%)."
4. **Literature use:** None identified in the recovered verbatim text for this passage.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as directly demonstrated, own-model-grounded fact (a topographic/geomorphic feature visible in the paper's own site data), not hedged.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The riverine exception (a decrease for one specific scenario/time-window/return-period combination, against an otherwise consistent increase pattern) is named explicitly within the same sentence rather than omitted or folded into the general "increase" statement — a concise instance of not suppressing a discordant sub-result.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — reinforces that flood-risk drivers and their relative importance are highly site-specific (topography-dependent for coastal; less clear-cut for riverine), a theme that becomes explicit in the paper's cross-city framing.
8. **Limitations:** None named in this specific passage.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, site-specific mechanistic explanation + explicit exception-reporting.

#### §7 — Oarsoaldea paragraph(s)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — reports the smallest coastal-flood impact of the three cities, attributes it to port engineering design, validates the general storm-surge-projection pattern against external literature, and reports a riverine-flood decrease (the only city showing a consistent decrease).
2. **Finding it starts from:** Oarsoaldea's small coastal design-basis water levels relative to the port's 2 m tidal-excursion design tolerance, and small (≤3%) coastal flood-volume change; riverine flood decrease.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct factual statement of the port's design basis and the modeled extreme values, confirmed verbatim: "For the city of Oarsoaldea the port area has been designed to face tidal excursions around 2 m. The extreme values associated with both 25 and 100 years RP range between 0.395 and 0.486 m" — a direct comparative-magnitude move (design tolerance vs. projected extreme), similar in structure to Nguyen (Ca Mau)'s road-crest-elevation-vs-projected-water-level comparison in the original 9-paper file, but here showing a comfortable safety margin rather than a projected exceedance. Concordance with prior literature is then stated directly: "Table 5 reports increases... and decreases... of the extreme water level for the projections compared to the Historical, **consistent with the findings of** Vousdoukas et al. (2017)." Riverine flooding is reported plainly: "a generalized decrease in peak Q_RP values is observed," with a corresponding underestimation caveat structurally parallel to the Massa riverine paragraph: "an expected slight underestimation of the projected extremes."
4. **Literature use:** Vousdoukas et al. (2017) used for direct concordance validation of the mixed-sign extreme-water-level pattern across scenarios — an A1-type move.
5. **Certainty level:** The port-design comparison and coastal-volume figures are stated as direct fact; the riverine underestimation caveat is stated as an "expected" (hedged) pattern, mirroring the same hedge used for Massa's riverine result, suggesting this hedge is a general property of the paper's discharge-extreme-value methodology rather than a site-specific concern.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Concordant (Vousdoukas et al. 2017 validation).
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — Oarsoaldea functions as the paper's "success case" for engineered coastal protection, useful as a contrast against Massa's much larger projected increases.
8. **Limitations:** The riverine underestimation caveat is a locally placed, precisely worded limitation, structurally identical to the same caveat given for Massa.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, comparative-magnitude (engineering design standard) + concordance-validating.

#### §7 — Model-limitations paragraphs (DEM resolution, wave-runup formula, compound-event simplification, tidal-regime stationarity assumption)
1. **Function:** A sequence of explicit, topically distinct methodological limitations, each naming a specific simplifying assumption in the modeling chain and (for at least the DEM and compound-event cases) its consequence for the reliability of the results — structurally similar to Nguyen (Ca Mau, Paper 7 in the original file)'s sequential itemized-limitations block late in its Discussion.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own modeling-chain design choices (DEM resolution ~20 m for the regional-to-urban transition; empirical wave-runup formula; independent, non-interacting treatment of storm surge and river discharge; fixed or simplified tidal boundary conditions).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Each limitation is named directly and its specific consequence stated: DEM — confirmed verbatim, "A coarse starting DEM of around 20 m resolution cannot even resolve streets and spaces between buildings, potentially blocking the flow and significantly changing the flooding pattern" (a precise, mechanism-level statement of *how* the limitation degrades results, not a vague caveat); wave runup — the empirical-formula choice "introduces uncertainties due to the degree of alongshore variability of the beach or due to the reduced knowledge of the underwater bathymetry" (per the earlier summarized pass; exact full sentence structure `[KHÔNG XÁC ĐỊNH ĐƯỢC — could not confirm complete verbatim wording via WebFetch, which repeatedly declined full paragraph reproduction for this passage]`); compound events — "we consider non-interacting storm surges and river discharges. Such a choice is aimed at simplifying the approach and having greater control on each driver of a flood event" (per the summarized pass; full verbatim paragraph `[KHÔNG XÁC ĐỊNH ĐƯỢC]`); tidal regime — confirmed verbatim for the final two Discussion paragraphs: "Accounting for the tide by adding a fixed Δη_Tide to the extreme event hydrograph (Massa and Villanova), or by simulating a semidiurnal tide (Oarsoaldea) as a boundary condition, can overlook the long-term (century-scale) modifications in tidal ranges," followed by an explicit, balanced closing reflection: "On the one hand, we are making a strong assumption, considering the surrounding environment does not change over time. On the other hand, the knowledge of specific characteristics of the analyzed area are crucial in modeling the impact of flood events."
4. **Literature use:** None identified in the recovered text for this block.
5. **Certainty level:** Each limitation is stated plainly as an acknowledged, known modeling-choice consequence, not hedged as uncertain about whether the limitation exists; a general, cross-cutting statement about the modeling chain's compounding error (recovered via the earlier summarized pass, not confirmed full-verbatim: "the errors accumulating throughout the modeling chains are difficult to estimate and are the results of unavoidable approximations") functions as an overarching, unhedged acknowledgment that total error is not quantifiable, only individually-sourced errors are nameable.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None explicit in the tidal-regime closing paragraph — it ends on a balanced, two-sided methodological reflection ("on the one hand... on the other hand...") rather than a synthesis, a forward-research call, or a practical/policy recommendation.
8. **Limitations bounding:** This is the paper's most limitation-dense block, four distinct, topically separate limitations in sequence (DEM resolution, runup-formula uncertainty, compound-event non-interaction, tidal-regime stationarity), ending the Discussion entirely on the last of these with no subsequent synthesis paragraph.
9. **Role:** Limitations, interleaved and then terminal — the Discussion's last recovered content ends on the tidal-stationarity limitation's two-sided reflection, with Section 8 (Conclusion and outlook) beginning immediately after with a restatement of the paper's overall aim ("In this work we present a modeling chain to transfer synoptic scale atmospheric information...") rather than any further Discussion-internal synthesis. This is a clear instance of a Discussion ending directly on an unresolved/balanced methodological limitation, structurally matching the A4b non-conforming pattern already identified in Rodrigues do Amaral, Luu, and (functionally) Nguyen (Ca Mau) in the original 9-paper file.

**Note on this paper's structural character:** Bendoni et al. is a multi-city, multi-scenario, multi-model comparative Discussion whose organizing logic is per-city (not per-mechanism or per-comparator-study) — each city gets its own short block of interpretation, mirroring (at a smaller scale) the multi-site organizing structure also seen in Chounna Yemele (Douala) and Strozzi et al. (Paper 10, above), though here further subdivided into coastal vs. riverine findings within each city. A1 (concordance-first validation) appears twice, both against a single named external comparator (IPCC AR6; Vousdoukas et al. 2017), each doing real interpretive work (resolving an apparent puzzle; validating a mixed-sign pattern) rather than being a generic "consistent with the literature" gesture. The Discussion's limitation content is unusually front-loaded (paragraphs 1-2, immediately) AND back-loaded (the final four-limitation block) — limitations bookend the city-specific interpretive core rather than being interleaved evenly throughout, a distinct structural variant from the corpus norm of continuous local interleaving.
## PAPER 12: Dini, Lacroix & Doin 2026 — Achoma landslide, Peru, InSAR precursors

**Citation:** Dini, B., Lacroix, P., and Doin, M.-P. (2026). *Beyond and beneath displacement time series: towards InSAR-based early warnings and deformation analysis of the Achoma landslide, Peru.* Nat. Hazards Earth Syst. Sci., 26, 863– (2026). URL: https://nhess.copernicus.org/articles/26/863/2026/

**Status: SUCCESS.** Full research article confirmed. Complete verbatim Discussion text recovered for all five subsections (5.1–5.5) via targeted WebFetch calls, citations intact throughout. Standalone Discussion (`5 Discussion`), separate from `4 Results`, followed by `6 Conclusions`.

### STEP 1 — Foundations
- **Research question:** Can interferometric coherence (rather than unwrapped displacement time series, which are known to underestimate landslide motion) function as a reliable spatial and temporal marker of slope destabilization and its critical acceleration phase? Can a dimensionless activity index derived from wrapped-phase data (again avoiding the unwrapping step) reveal precursory kinematic behavior even when displacement time series are unreliable?
- **Objective:** Reconstruct the multi-year deformation history of the Achoma landslide (Peru, catastrophic failure 18 June 2020) using alternative InSAR signal markers — coherence maps, a coherence ratio (landslide area vs. surrounding stable area), and a dimensionless wrapped-phase activity index — to identify precursory signals up to 5 years before failure, and assess their value for early-warning application, including cross-referencing against external forcing (seismicity, rainfall, river erosion).
- **Study design:** Empirical single-case retrospective InSAR reconstruction. 514 Sentinel-1 wrapped interferograms from 114 acquisitions (April 2015–June 2020, ascending track), multilooked to 18.4 m x 28.2 m; topographic-error correction; coherence-loss analysis (spatial and temporal, landslide vs. surroundings); wrapped-phase dimensionless activity index; 219 PlanetScope optical images (3 m resolution) for independent river-erosion analysis; cross-referenced against earthquake peak-ground-acceleration records and rainfall data.
- **Population/sample:** Not a human population — single landslide case study (Achoma, Colca Valley, Peru): ~800 m scarp width, 500 m downslope extent, ~0.4 km² area, ~20x10^6 m³ volume, ~100 m headscarp height, ~50 m average rupture-surface depth; unnoticed by residents until 1 month before failure.
- **Key findings the Discussion must interpret:**
  - Low-coherence lineaments visible up to ~5 years before failure, tracing the landslide's eventual boundary.
  - Coherence ratio (landslide/surroundings) stable near 0.98 for ~5 years, then a sharp drop beginning ~4 March 2020 (about 3.5 months before failure), crossing a retrospective 1-SD threshold 9-21 April 2020 (~2 months before failure), falling to 0.19 at failure.
  - Four distinct activity phases identified via the dimensionless wrapped-phase index (Nov 2017-Apr 2018; Jun 2018-May 2019; Jul 2019; Jan-Mar 2020), with escalating displacement rates and a final phase that does not recover to a steady state before failure (unlike phases 1-2).
  - External forcing (August 2016 earthquake — highest PGA in the record; comparable rainfall totals in multiple years; no clear river-erosion trend at the toe) did not reliably predict the timing of acceleration phases — comparable triggers in different years produced different responses, implicating internal (accumulated damage / basal shear plane maturation) rather than purely external drivers.

### STEP 2 — Discussion paragraph-by-paragraph

#### §5.1 "Spatial patterns of low coherence" — Paragraph 1 (only paragraph in this short subsection, though functionally two thematic parts)
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — establishes the paper's central novel claim (coherence maps reveal destabilization structures years before failure) directly from the earliest available data, then broadens the claim's evidentiary base via a within-region generalization to other landslides, including landslides that did NOT fail catastrophically.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The earliest interferogram (30 Apr-11 Nov 2015) already showing a low-coherence boundary matching the landslide's eventual final shape.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct declarative interpretation, unhedged in the main clause but immediately qualified with an inferential hedge for the underlying cause: "The low-coherence markers **likely indicate** areas of strain resulting from landslide displacement, marking the boundary between stable and unstable ground." The claim is then extended: "We observed similar features in other landslides in the Colca Valley, **indicating that** these signals are not isolated occurrences." A discordant/complicating sub-finding is folded in without disruption: some of those other landslides "exhibited long-term creep without progressing to catastrophic failure" — meaning the marker is not by itself a predictor of eventual catastrophic collapse, only of destabilization onset, a distinction the paragraph does not shy away from stating.
4. **Literature use:** None in this specific paragraph — the generalization is supported by the paper's own regional survey (other Colca Valley landslides), not external citations.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly hedged at the mechanistic-attribution level ("likely indicate") — an appropriately cautious claim since coherence loss is an indirect proxy for strain, not a direct displacement measurement; the broader regional-pattern claim is stated with more confidence, grounded in the paper's own multi-site observation.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** The non-catastrophic creeping landslides are a genuine complicating case for the paper's early-warning framing (the marker fires for slopes that never catastrophically fail), and the paragraph states this plainly rather than omitting it — an implicit acknowledgment that the marker indicates destabilization-onset, not failure-certainty.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — establishes that this marker generalizes beyond the single case study, motivating the "large-scale early detection" framing that recurs in §5.5.
8. **Limitations:** None named explicitly yet, though the non-failing-landslide observation functions as an implicit scope qualifier on what the marker can and cannot predict.
9. **Role:** Opening/core interpretive for the Discussion, establishing the central novel claim with an immediate, honest complication.

#### §5.2 "Factors influencing coherence loss" — Paragraph 1
1. **Function:** Rival-hypothesis elimination — explicitly lists the generic causes of coherence loss known from the wider literature, then argues why the alternative, non-landslide-related causes are implausible for this specific case, before committing to the deformation-based interpretation.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The general phenomenon of interferometric coherence loss and its multiple possible causes.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Lists generic causes first (vegetation, crops, debris, snow, deformation heterogeneity) as an evenhanded background statement, then explicitly narrows: "In the case of the Achoma landslide, the low coherence markers observed are **unlikely to be caused by** localised land cover changes. Instead, they **likely reflect** slight differential displacements..." — a direct elimination-then-commitment structure (C1-type move), though the elimination itself is not deeply evidenced within this sentence (no specific land-cover data cited to rule it out), making it a more asserted than demonstrated elimination.
4. **Literature use:** Lacroix et al. (2023) supports a direct concordance/timing claim: "These features have been observed up to 3 months before failure, **similar to** the final acceleration stage detected with optical images by Lacroix et al. (2023)" — cross-method (InSAR coherence vs. optical) concordance validation, an especially strong form of A1 since the comparator uses an independent measurement technique on the same landslide, not just a different site. Jacquemart and Tiampo (2021) is then cited to acknowledge that "distinguishing between these causes is challenging" for coherence loss generally — a candid citation admitting the paper's own attribution (differential displacement, not land cover) cannot be definitively isolated from other contributing factors, even as the paper commits to it as the primary explanation.
5. **Certainty level:** Explicitly hedged for the primary causal claim ("unlikely to be caused by," "likely reflect") — appropriately tentative given the paper's own citation (Jacquemart and Tiampo 2021) states the causes are inherently hard to disentangle.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** Fully concordant with the Lacroix et al. (2023) optical-method timing.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Sets up Paragraph 2's transition-detection framing as a lead-time/early-warning implication.
8. **Limitations:** The explicit citation of Jacquemart and Tiampo (2021) on attribution difficulty functions as a locally placed, honest limitation on the causal claim's certainty.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, rival-hypothesis elimination (partial/asserted rather than fully evidenced) + cross-method concordance validation.

#### §5.2 — Paragraph 2
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — interprets the localized-to-widespread coherence-loss transition as a kinematic regime shift, and draws a direct forward-looking practical implication (early-warning lead time).
2. **Finding it starts from:** The March 2020 transition from localized coherence anomalies to widespread loss across the whole landslide area.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct interpretive statement, moderately hedged: "points to **significant shift** in landslide dynamics. **This likely marks** the transition from small displacements (with the landslide behaving as a cohesive body) to higher displacement gradients and internal deformation."
4. **Literature use:** None new in this paragraph.
5. **Certainty level:** Moderately hedged ("likely marks") — appropriate for a mechanistic interpretation of an observed pattern change, not a directly measured mechanism.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Direct and explicit: "Identifying such transitions in coherence could provide valuable lead time for early warnings, offering a proactive approach to landslide monitoring in previously unmonitored areas" — the paper's first explicit practical/applied implication statement, appropriately hedged ("could provide") since it is a prospective, not yet operationally demonstrated, capability claim.
8. **Limitations:** None new.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, closing §5.2 with a practical-implication move.

#### §5.3 "Coherence ratio analysis" — Paragraph 1
1. **Function:** Methodological framing — explains why the coherence-ratio approach (rather than raw coherence) is used, attributing the underlying design logic to a prior study.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The paper's own choice to compute a landslide-to-surroundings coherence ratio.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct methodological justification: "This approach, **similar to** Jacquemart and Tiampo (2021), helps account for temporal coherence variability affecting both the landslide and surroundings similarly." No hedge — stated as an established methodological rationale.
4. **Literature use:** Jacquemart and Tiampo (2021) cited to support the paper's own methodological design choice — a "prior-method-adopted-and-applied" literature use, distinct from concordance/discordance-of-findings uses elsewhere.
5. **Certainty level:** Stated as established methodological reasoning (a normalization logic), not hedged.
6. **Concordant/discordant:** N/A.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** None yet — pure methodological framing.
8. **Limitations:** None.
9. **Role:** Orienting/methodological, opening §5.3.

#### §5.3 — Paragraph 2
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — reports the headline coherence-ratio-drop finding with a precise lead-time quantification, then validates the finding's general plausibility (a pre-failure coherence-ratio decline is a known phenomenon) against an external comparator while explicitly naming a discordance in the specific lead time.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The coherence-ratio drop beginning ~4 March 2020, crossing a 1-SD threshold 9-21 April 2020 (~2 months predictive lead time before the 18 June 2020 failure).
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct, quantified statement of the paper's own finding and its predictive-lead-time framing ("indicating predictive value about 2 months before the Achoma failure"), immediately followed by a comparator citation that both validates the general phenomenon and reveals a magnitude/timing discordance: "Jacquemart and Tiampo (2021) noted **similar** coherence ratio decline **approximately 5 months before** the Mudcreek landslide, attributing it partly to vegetation degradation." The paper does not reconcile the 2-month (Achoma) vs. 5-month (Mudcreek) discrepancy explicitly within this paragraph — it is stated as a bare comparative fact, leaving the reader to note the site-specific difference in predictive lead time without further explanation at this point (partially resolved later in §5.5's Complementarity discussion).
4. **Literature use:** Jacquemart and Tiampo (2021) — a genuinely mixed concordant/discordant use within one citation: concordant on the general phenomenon (coherence-ratio decline before failure), discordant/unreconciled on the specific quantitative lead time and partly on attributed cause (vegetation degradation there, vs. differential displacement/phase aliasing at Achoma).
5. **Certainty level:** The paper's own finding is stated as directly measured fact (dates, threshold values); the comparison to Mudcreek is presented factually without a confidence qualifier on the comparison itself.
6. **Concordant/discordant handling:** A genuine unreconciled discordance in magnitude (2 vs. 5 months) is stated plainly, not smoothed into a vague "consistent with other studies" claim — the paper lets the numbers speak without forcing false equivalence.
7. **Interpretation→implication:** Implicit — the predictive-lead-time framing directly supports the early-warning-application argument threaded throughout the Discussion.
8. **Limitations:** None explicit yet.
9. **Role:** Core interpretive, comparative with an explicitly unreconciled discordant sub-element (magnitude/timing mismatch) — structurally close to the B2-type dual-use-of-one-citation pattern from Shahi et al. (Paper 2, original 9-paper file), here spanning two sub-uses across two consecutive sentences of the same paragraph.

#### §5.3 — Paragraph 3
1. **Function:** Core interpretive — explains a further, post-failure discordant/counter-directional observation (coherence ratio INCREASES sharply after failure), resolves it mechanistically, then validates the pre-failure interpretation against the same independent optical-method comparator used in §5.2, and closes with an explicit call for further cross-site work.
2. **Finding it starts from:** The post-failure coherence-ratio increase, up to 1.98 during the 2021 rainy season — the opposite direction from the pre-failure decline.
3. **Mechanism of the move:** Direct contrastive framing: "**In contrast**, the Achoma analysis shows a sharp increase in the coherence ratio post-failure..." This apparent reversal is explained mechanistically, moderately hedged: "**This increase suggests that** vegetation degradation following the failure **may expose** rock and soil, enhancing phase stability in the absence of displacements." The paragraph then closes the loop on the pre-failure interpretation: "**Thus**, the observed drop in coherence leading up to failure is **likely associated with** high displacement gradients... and phase aliasing. **This observation is consistent with** the 3-month final acceleration stage identified through independent measurements by Lacroix et al. (2023)" — a second cross-method concordance validation (optical, independent of this paper's own InSAR analysis) for the same 3-month critical window already validated in §5.2.
4. **Literature use:** Lacroix et al. (2023) re-cited for a second, independent concordance check (the 3-month critical-transition timing) — reinforcing the paper's central finding via repeated cross-validation against the same external, methodologically independent source, rather than introducing a new citation each time.
